34 Years Later, Clint Eastwood's Best Quote Is Still the Greatest in Western Movie History

📅 Nov 28, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

For many who grew up in the 1990s, Unforgiven was the definitive Western. It somehow smelled of whiskey, gunsmoke, and a father's aftershave, and remained a monolithic genre presence for years to come — that's how praised it was and how ubiquitous it became on network television after its release. While director, producer, composer, and star Clint Eastwood actually waffled on making the film for years, once he did, he defined the modern revisionist Western and reminded audiences of his incredible talent, while looking back at his career with clear eyes.

Eastwood's last true Western proved to be arguably his finest, while also brutally reassessing the bloodthirsty and heroic clichés he'd embodied for decades. Unforgiven is one of the most melancholic Westerns ever made outside of Italy, and Eastwood's character is given multiple lines and moments when it's clear he's both delivering the character's dialogue and speaking directly to the violence that defined his cinematic career. And while the film is replete with unforgettable moments, his exchange with a naive aspiring gunslinger is arguably the final word on the life of a gunslinger and the genre itself.

In a film full of heartbreaking moments (aided in no small part by Eastwood and Lennie Niehaus's guitar-forward score), few make such an elemental impression as Eastwood's former gunslinger William Munny consoling the brash but inexperienced "Schofield Kid" (Jaimz Woolvett) after he makes his first kill. The Kid has been bragging for half the film about how many men he's shot down, but after Munny presses him into basically executing a man in an outhouse as they chase a bounty, the Kid breaks down. This was his first.

Whiskey and self-reassurance that it's "like it was in the old days," imagining Munny's former profession as romantically as he can, are all the Kid has to hold it together. Eastwood just stares out at the town of Big Whiskey, where he knows there's still killing to be done, and tells him to just take another drink.

"It don't seem real — how you can never breathe again, never. How he's dead ... all on account of pulling a trigger," the Kid breaks down. "It's a hell of a thing, killing a man. Take away all he's got and all he's ever gonna have," Munny replies. And when the Kid insists that their victims "had it coming," Munny just adds: "We all got it coming, kid."

These flicks will entertain just about anybody.

After his spaghetti Western period, with all its stylish, bloodthirsty trappings, and even his own 1973 film High Plains Drifter (in which Eastwood allowed himself to be a quasi-supernatural rapist and mass murderer), Unforgiven can't help but feel like a pause and a look back. It's Eastwood saying goodbye to the genre he remade in his image and reckoning with the fascistic, wanton killing that defined the films that made him Hollywood royalty. Goodbye, Man With No Name and goodbye, Dirty Harry. It's no coincidence that Eastwood would move into a much more classicist auteur phase with his later films, and engage openly and poignantly on topics from the early (and ongoing) sins of the FBI to the horror of the ordinary Japanese soldier at Iwo Jima.

It doesn't hurt that Unforgiven is also a visual and technical masterpiece, with Eastwood genuinely syncing his onscreen persona with his role as director; not a shot is wasted and everything reeks of regret. The film is a widescreen masterpiece that uses weather to its greatest effect. Gene Hackman may ham it up a bit with the cruelty of Munny's target, Little Bill, making it genuinely chilling when he mixes his folksy affect with sudden violence — but when he begs Munny for his life, insisting that he really just wanted to keep building his house, it's as sad as a villain's end can get.

Unforgiven earned Eastwood four well-deserved Oscars, and it hasn't aged a day. While his vast cinematic output is full of certified bangers even in 2026, there's nothing that hits quite as hard as his first true look back on things. Even modern Western masterpieces like the Coen Brothers' beautifully constructed, hilarious and heartbreaking take on True Grit don't have the same weathered soul to them and don't pierce the heart the same way. Eastwood's last word on the genre proved to be just that for all of us.

Jon Bernthal’s 132-Minute Crime Thriller 'The Accountant 2' Is the Perfect Watch Before It’s Cut From Hulu

📅 Nov 28, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

It can’t always be award-winning dramas and true-crime documentaries when you’re watching movies at home. Sometimes, you want an entertaining, bullet-riddled rollercoaster, and there are few that fit the bill better than 2025’s The Accountant 2. In the long-awaited sequel to the 2016 original, Ben Affleck returns as Christian Wolff, a neurodivergent accountant and trained killer who comes out of hiding to solve a case involving the death of an old friend, which forces him to reunite with his estranged brother, played by Jon Bernthal. The pair make for a unique buddy movie pairing, filled with action and withering put-downs that made the movie a hit with audiences. With the film leaving Hulu on December 1, your last chance to watch it streaming is November 30.

When Ray King (J.K. Simmons), Christian’s ally and government contact, is murdered and found with the cryptic message “find the accountant” on his arm, treasury agent Marybeth Medina (Cynthia Addai-Robinson) seeks out Christian for help. Keen to avenge his friend, he links the killings to a large conspiracy involving the disappearance of a family and dangerous criminals. Realizing he can’t do this alone, he contacts his estranged brother Brax (Bernthal) to help him. With a difficult past between them, the siblings reluctantly come together to piece together the clues and find justice.

Part mystery thriller, part buddy cop movie, the film features the two stars taking on a variety of bad guys as well as solving numerous puzzles to find their target. While undoubtedly a traditional action movie, there’s also a fair amount of laughs to be had as the Wolff boys slowly begin to trust each other again.

Much of the fun of this sequel is in watching the brothers try to co-exist, and seeing their seemingly opposite personalities lead to some hilarious clashes. Christian is methodical, controlled, and able to point out the smallest details, despite having difficulties with social interaction. By contrast, Brax is emotional, abrasive, and constantly seeking connection. He bellows where Christian mutters, meaning that as partners and family members, it can be difficult to find common ground.

While a lazier movie might have positioned Christian’s neurodivergence as making him the outsider, the enjoyment comes from neither brother being the wildcard. Both characters’ personalities have their strengths and weaknesses, and as the story progresses, there’s a sense of satisfaction in the pair finding out that they may have more in common than they realize. It’s in these interactions where Bernthal becomes the movie’s secret weapon. He has a talent for comedy that might not immediately be obvious given his numerous action-heavy roles, but he's adept at highlighting the ridiculousness of his situation, able to use himself for laughs as well as his co-stars.

The first movie was about uncovering who Christian is, given he has built a life where his footprints are covered and his identity protected. However, The Accountant 2 doesn’t have to do the same amount of digging, making this a movie that can almost immediately dive into the action. With the audience being aware of what Christian is capable of, there’s a shorthand that allows the plot to move forward in a swifter way, and gives him a literal brother in arms.

With a growing number of villains on their tail, the action scenes are more frequent, and evolve as the brothers’ relationship becomes stronger. Christian treats all things in life like an equation, with physical altercations being no different. On the other hand, Brax operates on instinct, able to react and change course depending on what the situation calls for. These two approaches slowly begin to complement each other, leading to a terrific finale where they penetrate a heavily armed compound, outnumbered but never outsmarted.

The Accountant 2 moves the formula of the first movie forward in a way that will satisfy fans who want to see more of what they liked from before, but with the volume turned up. WriterBill Dubuque revealed this year that the film’s central pairing was so successful, that there is interest in a third movie focusing on a road trip between Christian and Brax. If that sounds like something you’d be excited about, make sure you catch their latest adventure before it leaves your Hulu menu.

Laxman Utekar, Rajkumar Periasamy and Vishnuvardhan Set New Films With Bhushan Kumar’s T-Series (EXCLUSIVE)

📅 Nov 27, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Directors Laxman Utekar, Rajkumar Periasamy and Vishnuvardhan are set to collaborate with Indian studio T-Series chair and managing director Bhushan Kumar on three new films.

Title, story, cast and production details have not been disclosed yet.

Utekar directed the 2025 hit Hindi-language historical drama “Chhaava.” Periasamy won the best director prize at India’s National Film Awards for Tamil-language military biopic “Amaran,” while Vishnuvardhan’s Hindi-language war film “Shershaah” took the event’s Special Jury Award. Related Stories Chiwetel Ejiofor on How Stephen Frears’ 'Dirty Pretty Things' Led Him to Fall in Love With Film Jesse Eisenberg Says Fans Aren’t Crazy With Him Because ‘There’s No Sexuality to My Work’: Life Is ‘Different’ When People ‘Want to Sleep With’ You

The three collaborations add to a busy T-Series pipeline that runs across Bollywood and South Indian cinema, with leading stars from both industries attached. Several of those titles are produced by T-Series, while others are presented by the company.

Under its own production banner, T-Series is backing Sandeep Reddy Vanga’s “Spirit,” which stars Prabhas, and an untitled Anurag Basu film with Kartik Aaryan. Also on the roster are Ram Gopal Varma’s “Police Company” and an untitled Milap Zaveri project pairing Aditya Roy Kapur and Tara Sutaria.

Kumar is producing “The Pride of Bharat – Chhatrapati Shivaji Maharaj,” a historical drama in two parts from director Sandeep Singh, with “Kantara” star Rishab Shetty in the lead. Aaryan has a second film on the slate, Shimit Amin’s “Captain India.” “Khosla Ka Ghosla 2,” “Karnika” and “Jaani Dushman” round out the list.

In the south, T-Series is presenting Hanu Raghavapudi’s “Fauzi,” which gives Prabhas a second title on the slate, and Prasanth Varma’s mythological feature “Jai Hanuman.” The company is also presenting Prashanth Neel’s “Dragon,” led by Jr NTR, and Sukumar’s next film with Ram Charan, which reunites director and star.

In all, the slate takes in action, romance, period drama and mythology, and spans both the Bollywood and South Indian industries.

Anya Taylor-Joy’s 2-Hour Thriller Is Still Her Greatest Movie 10 Years Later

📅 Nov 27, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Anya Taylor-Joy’s film debut in Robert Eggers’ The Witch may have given the actor a cult following, but it was her role in M. Night Shyamalan’s Split in 2016 that made her a household name. A year after she shocked horror fans as Thomasin, she took on an incredibly different role in the thriller. A return to form for Shyamalan, Split takes on the complex issue of DID. Dissociative Identity Disorder is at the crux of the movie as James McAvoy takes on 24 separate identities in his role of Kevin.

Viewers only get to see some of them, but his immense talent in showing just a few is one of the best examples of character work. Even more impressive is Taylor-Joy, who stands toe to toe with the performer in her role as Casey Cooke. One day when Casey is out at a birthday party, she and acquaintances Marcia (Jessica Sula) and Claire (Haley Lu Richardson) are abducted by some of Kevin’s most insidious personalities. In the locked room drama, Casey weaponizes her wits and her own experience with trauma to escape her captivity and save her life.

M. Night Shyamalan features have typically been divisive in recent years, and Split is no different. In the movie, the director posits that DID can present itself not just in personality changes, but with physical characteristics as well. One of the most notorious of these personalities, the Beast, has more muscle definition and becomes literally bigger than the other personalities. This may be controversial with the mental health community, but there is no denying that this offers a fascinating new perspective on the subject of trauma.

Kevin materialized these personalities as a response to his overbearing and abusive mother. His foil in the film is Casey, played by Anya Taylor-Joy. Immediately, it is clear that Casey has a superpower that the other abductees do not. Slowly throughout the film, Casey’s own abuse and trauma are revealed, which allows her to empathize with Kevin. While Marcia and Claire want to break their way out by force, Casey understands that the best way to escape is through intelligence and subtlety.

This is a difficult balance to maintain, but Taylor-Joy does it almost effortlessly. She establishes this in the abduction scene as it slowly dawns on Casey that there is no escape. When Kevin’s personality, Dennis, enters the girls’ car, he subdues the other two easily in the backseat. Casey, who is in the front, immediately understands the danger in this encounter. As a single tear rolls down her face, she knows that there is no point in fighting and is taken as well.

What could be considered weakness or a fault in her character is exactly the opposite. Casey knows that she is a survivor. She has endured one monster before and will find a way to survive this one as well. Casey is characterized by her creativity in finding a way out of horrifying situations, which aids her until the very end of the movie. Just like in The Witch, Taylor-Joy accepts the task of a difficult character that could be misunderstood and instead makes it iconic. In the decade since Split first premiered, no other character has quite reached the heights of this one.

38-Episode ‘Reacher Meets Justified’ Masterpiece Has Hands Down The Best Action On TV

📅 Nov 27, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Prime Video’s Reacher appears to have set a new standard for pulsating, high-stakes action on the small screen, but it’s actually standing on the shoulder of another giant in the genre. Nine years before Alan Ritchson made his debut as Jack Reacher on streaming, a criminally underexposed Cinemax crime thriller was already wowing viewers lucky enough to have discovered it with jawdropping action sequences.

Centering on a mercenary outlaw who just happens to have found himself a sheriff’s badge in Pennsylvania, this show combines the heavy-hitting, close-quarters combat of Reacher with the swaggering, Western-inflected thrills of Justified. It takes pride of place among the best action TV series ever made, despite not having nearly the same profile of bigger names in the genre.

There are even those who’d go as far as claiming outright that the show is even better than Reacher at what it does. When it comes to sheer immersion in the art of painstakingly choreographed violence, this opinion stands to reason. What’s more, Banshee has a brilliantly original premise, dreamed up by Your Friends & Neighbors creator Jonathan Tropper and bestselling author David Schickler.

The means by which Lucas Hood assumes his identity as sheriff of the show’s titular Pennsylvania town is steeped in the traditions of neo-noir. What happens next grips us relentlessly across four seasons, punctuated by many of the greatest combat set-pieces ever made for television.

Anyone who enjoys the sight of Alan Ritchson working his way through a punishing action sequence in Reacher needs to find a way to watch Banshee on HBO and HBO Max, at the earliest possible opportunity. The Boys actor Antony Starr leaves Ritchson for dust playing an unnamed career criminal posing as lawman Lucas Hood.

We see Starr’s Banshee protagonist engage in all-out, full-throttle action often for several minutes per scene, while camera close-ups and near-invisible cuts in the edit give up little room to breathe. At the same time, the martial arts choreography by stuntman Marcus Young is some of the most authentic ever brought to the screen.

Before The Boys and Homelander, Antony Starr was in four seasons of a crime drama that deserved to be as big as ABC's and Nathan Fillion's The Rookie.

For the majority of each hour-long episode of Banshee, viewers are placed right in the thick of the action, which mixes steel and grit for the perfect action spectacular in a small-town setting. By the end credits, most viewers will find their heart racing at lightning pace. Bingewatch this series at your peril.

As a series that delivers non-stop action and keeps you on the edge of your seat throughout its run, Banshee is almost unrivaled in modern television. If a filmmaker managed to execute a car fight sequence as good as the one in the show’s third season, for example, they’d surely be in line for an Oscar nod. Yet, Banshee is still underappreciated by the television industry, and hasn’t been seen by nearly enough viewers.

If Banshee makes Reacher look tame to some audiences, then its Prime Video rival at least owes a debt of gratitude to the path it forged over on Cinemax during the mid-2010s. Like Jack Reacher, Lucas Hood is effectively a lone-wolf vigilante having to stave off all sorts of threats to the new life he’s made for himself in rural Pennsylvania.

However, whereas Reacher is ostensibly outside the law yet acts in the interests of good and adheres to a strict moral code, Hood is superficially a legitimate lawman but hides a much darker identity beneath his sheriff’s badge. His approach to running local law enforcement also owes something to maverick U.S. Marshal Raylan Givens from Justified.

Like Givens, Hood has to contend with the sorts of dangerous and unsavory characters who find their way through the cracks of rural areas in the Eastern United States. These characters include his nemesis, ex-Amish crime cartel boss Kai Proctor, played menacingly by Ulrich Thomsen.

Banshee is a compelling piece of storytelling, even leaving aside the visual mastery of its action sequences. It deserves not only the acclaim that Reacher and Justified have both received, but a similarly impressive popular following, too.

'The Last of Us' Meets 'Silo' in Hulu’s 4-Part Sci-Fi Thriller

📅 Nov 27, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Post-apocalyptic sci-fi thrillers have become some of TV's best shows. With examples like The Last of Us or Silo, these stories have grown incredibly popular for their spectacle and epic tales. The genre is more than gruesome stories about the world ending, as it offers a deep exploration of universal human struggles. While these recent examples have earned considerable attention, and rightfully so, they are not the first. In fact, Snowpiercer took a similar approach in 2020. The series creates an immersive world full of danger and division that all fans of the genre should watch.

Snowpiercer delivers a unique premise and incredible worldbuilding that sets it apart. Inspired by the 2013 film of the same name, the story isn't totally original, but the series builds on the concept that always deserved more than two hours. And Snowpiercer certainly expands the idea with four unforgettable seasons. Though it may not have gained the popularity that other comparable series have, it still deserves attention. Six years later, Snowpiercer is the perfect binge watch for anyone who loves sci-fi thrillers, dystopian worlds, or complex moral questions.

Snowpiercer features a world like no other, where a massive luxury train is the only place people can live. Eerily set in 2026, the story takes place seven years after an environmental disaster created a new ice age, killing much of human civilization. However, some survive on a constantly moving train designed by eccentric billionaire Mr. Joseph Wilford (Sean Bean). While the movement keeps them alive, the survivors continue to struggle. The system is strictly divided into distinct social classes. The elite live in the front, third-class laborers keep the machine running, and the stowaways, known as the Tailies, are just trying to survive. However, the dynamic alters when a series of murders threatens the train. Head Engineer Melanie Cavill (Jennifer Connelly) pulls Chicago police officer Andre Layton (Daveed Diggs) up from the Tail to uncover the truth.

Layton has his own motivations. He dreams of revolution and believes this investigation can take him one step closer, allowing him to map out the train. However, the fallout spirals as the train is full of secrets. Most prominently, the passengers begin to realize that inventor Joseph Wilford isn't running the train because Melanie abandoned him during boarding, leaving him for dead. Although this society is confined to a train, Snowpiercer covers a lot of ground as it explores the brutal realities of civilization, oppression, and unavoidable moral questions of survival. This is the crux of the series, giving it a dark and fascinating tone.

What stands out most about Snowpiercer is its worldbuilding. A train is an uncharacteristic setting, but it comes with plenty to explore as the series delves into how different biomes separate the classes, how food is grown, and even how the passengers deal with their dead. These may seem like small details, but they reveal a lot about the world and the desperate characters who inhabit it. Through these elements, the series brings this society to life and creates something new. As the story goes on, Snowpiercer even explores beyond the train, showing the devastation of the icy world and further immersing the audience in the dystopian setting.

Idris Elba has fans on the edges of their train seats.

Though this concept is rare, the story is inspired by the movie, but the two are far from the same. Of course, there are similarities, but Snowpiercer isn't even strictly a remake. The series takes the concept, but uses different characters and circumstances to tell its own story. With significantly more time, the series sets a slower pace, building tension and introducing the world a little at a time, which is a luxury the film doesn't have. Ultimately, that dedication to the story is what makes Snowpiercer work. The series stands out, even as the dystopian genre grows, and though it's a few years old now, Snowpiercer is an unmatched sci-fi watch that deserves more attention.

Snowpiercer is streaming on Hulu in the U.S.

Anya Taylor-Joy’s 93-Minute Crime Thriller 'Thoroughbreds' Is the Perfect Watch Before It’s Cut From Streaming

📅 Nov 27, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

One of the most unsung releases of the last decade was 2017’s Thoroughbreds, a thriller deep in the film noir mode with three great actors going toe to toe. With Anya Taylor-Joy, Olivia Cooke, and Anton Yelchin as the architects and participants of a murder plot, it’s less a crime movie and more of a character study, with director Cory Finley mining both pathos and extremely dark comedy from the premise. In an era where minimal-location movies about the sociopathy of the rich were frequently successful discourse machines (including Taylor-Joy’s 2022 vehicle The Menu), Thoroughbreds was a classically amoral story of kids whose wealth insulates them from consequences, yet you still root for them. The movie was unfortunately ahead of the curve with its note-perfect plotting and criminal ideation, as well as its cast.

While the late Anton Yelchin had found major success in movies like Green Room or even the modern Star Trek films, Cooke and Taylor-Joy were just getting started, some years away from the blockbuster extravaganzas of Dune and House of the Dragon. Coming some years before that, Thoroughbreds suggested great depth from all three actors, playing them against each other as they all fight for their own agendas, while also giving viewers the chance to identify with potentially evil teens. The movie is leaving Starz on November 30, but its study of youthful sociopathy and the tenuous nature of female friendship means it should remain a cult classic for years to come.

Thoroughbreds is a movie of interiors, specializing in the rich, old money homes of its protagonists Amanda (Olivia Cooke) and Lily (Anya Taylor-Joy), who live in suburban Connecticut. From the very beginning, when Amanda mercy-kills her crippled horse (leading to the title) in an unnerving moment, the movie keeps its distance from the characters, with director Cory Finley’s camera holding on scenes and moments for long stretches. Rather than the aspirational gleeful immorality of the classic teenage murderer movie Heathers, Thoroughbreds is methodical and ironic. The fancy decor, the emotional repression, and the basement tanning beds all go hand in hand with Amanda’s claims of an inability to feel anything. You might wonder if she’s just being an edgy teenager, especially after the horse euthanizing moment. But the movie’s visual language backs her up.

In a twist directly out of Alfred Hitchcock’s Strangers on a Train, the movie’s narrative begins to hinge on a murderous favor. Lily, effectively tasked by Amanda’s mom to befriend her troubled daughter, has an abusive stepfather named Mark (Paul Sparks), and Amanda, in her emotionless wisdom, presents a solution: kill him. The girls have a means of laundering the murder in the form of drug dealer Tim (Yelchin). Olivia Cooke’s portrayal of Amanda veers beyond creating an empathetic version of somebody suffering from an undisclosed mental illness, but it never goes into villain territory either. The movie’s cold and heavily adorned visual sensibility might have Yorgos Lanthimos-esque ironic distance from what you see, but it means that the relationship between the two girls becomes increasingly complex, in a way that it wouldn’t be if viewers were directly keyed into how the girls see each other.

Cory Finley had reportedly envisioned the movie as a play originally, and that feeling lingers in the film. The minimal cast and locations, as well as the long takes and reliance on dialogue, have the potential to feel theatrical, but the movie is sophisticated on the level of cinematic storytelling, commenting on its cast with its staging and using its widescreen compositions for both comedy and terror as chickens come home to roost. As he told IndieWire, the story began with the idea of a character who couldn’t feel anything. As the friend seeking to take advantage of that to help her own family situation, Anya Taylor-Joy’s Lily is effectively not much different.

The movie’s cinematic lineage is rich, drawing from Alfred Hitchcock (besides Strangers on a Train, his highly theatrical one-take experiment Rope, with its homoerotic murder conspiracy, has a clear line to Thoroughbreds) as well as ‘90s evil rich kid movies like Cruel Intentions, but with a much less moral stance. Peter Jackson’s true-crime classic Heavenly Creatures also feels like an influence, with the fantastical sequences substituted for cold stylization. When Amanda and Lily’s plans go awry, we see the truth of what the girls are willing to go through with, how calculating they can be with the other’s emotional headspace. A different movie about teenage murder conspirators might let them off the hook, but Amanda’s dreams of wild thoroughbred horses suggest that a cruel end is coming to everybody.

Steve Martin And John Candy's Classic Thanksgiving Movie Is A Paramount+ Streaming Hit

📅 Nov 27, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

John Hughes made some of the most beloved movies of all time in his eight directorial efforts, to the point where it's nearly impossible to rank the films he wrote and directed. Still, there are few movies that reach the specific balance of comedy with such an emotional punch as Hughes does in 1987's "Planes, Trains and Automobiles." This is a goofball movie that borders on the surreal, with some of the most bizarre imagery in a comedy movie, while also delivering some of the most heartbreaking and heartwarming moments that came out of the 1980s.

The film stars Steve Martin as Neal Page, an uptight marketing executive forced to be paired with Del Griffith (John Candy), a goofball salesman with a heart of gold, when their journey from New York City to Chicago is disrupted. What was meant to be just a short cab and plane ride home just in time for Thanksgiving ends up being a three-day odyssey full of shenanigans, attempted murder, and much more.

It's no wonder, then, that "Planes, Trains and Automobiles" is the No. 1 movie streaming on Paramount+ on Thanksgiving Day 2026. After all, there are plenty of movies about Christmas, to the point where it's easy to overlook Thanksgiving, and with good reason. For one, it's not really a holiday outside the U.S. and Canada (and even then, celebrated on different days), and the imagery is not dissimilar to Christmas anyway. Still, the best Thanksgiving movies deliver a very specific tone that's less about holiday magic and more about celebrating what we have despite the horrors of daily life. From the opening minutes of "Planes, Trains and Automobiles," it's clear this movie could not be set at any other time of year except during the chaos and anxiety of Thanksgiving.

The travel chaos that is quite unique to Thanksgiving is the crux of "Planes, Trains and Automobiles." The arrival of snowstorms that plague the East Coast of the continental United States, the huge flux of travelers, and the general anxiety and irritability of travelers around the country add to, well, a very stressful time of year. It's why Steve Martin's uptight businessman Neal is so relatable. The film simply nails the feeling of needing to interact with others and ask for their help while being utterly frustrated with having to deal with anyone at such a bad time. It's also why John Candy's performance is such a tightrope, because his character has to be annoying in addition to being charming. Del's not really someone you'd want as a travel companion, yet he is absolutely the best person you want as a friend.

That tricky balance is what makes the movie so memorable, and why John Candy's best line remains the best Thanksgiving movie quote ever. Neal and Del are forced to not only set aside their differences, but actually recognize the humanity in each other while acknowledging that they themselves are flawed. This is a highly empathetic film that's all about how we should find love and camaraderie even in unlikely companions. That is why the film so perfectly captures the spirit of Thanksgiving, because it's not about estranged family members, but rather complete strangers who must come to learn they need one another and that they should be grateful for what they bring into each other's lives. Togetherness is not just for family members, or even people you like, but also for strangers you come to appreciate despite the ways they annoy you.

6 YA Books That Are Better Than They Get Credit For

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Young adult fiction has produced some of the biggest literary phenomena of the 21st century, from The Hunger Games to Twilight, and this trend shows no signs of slowing. Even now, YA hits like Heartstopper and A Good Girl's Guide to Murder are fixtures on the bestseller lists.

However, there are also plenty of YA gems that are a little under-sung. Perhaps bigger franchises stole their limelight, or they had their moment in the sun but then mostly faded from popularity, or they received rave reviews but struggled to reach a wide, mainstream audience. With that in mind, here are a few titles that probably deserve a little more praise and attention than they get.

"Sometimes the most beautiful things are the most dangerous." This is a fast-paced, atmospheric fantasy novel. Our protagonist is 16-year-old orphan Juliette, who works tirelessly to gift her beloved older sister, Clare, a weeklong stay at The Splendor. It's a glamorous, magical hotel on a hill known for fulfilling its guests' wildest fantasies. However, Clare returns from the trip completely changed: cold and detached, unfeeling. Determined to uncover what broke their bond, Juliette checks into the hotel herself.

Inside, she meets Henri, a talented young illusionist employed by the hotel to manipulate guests' minds and memories to craft their "Signature Experience". As Juliette digs beneath the hotel's dazzling surface, Henri realizes he is directly tied to the heartache she is trying to undo. Their dual perspectives (alternating between viewpoint chapters) drive the mystery, while layered world-building and a nuanced magic system do the rest of the heavy lifting.

"This place is like the whole world and the whole world is like this place. So where could I go?" The Noughts & Crosses series ranks among the most thoughtful YA dystopian fiction of the last quarter-century, yet it's typically overshadowed by the likes of The Hunger Games. Set in an alternate society where Black people, known as Crosses, occupy positions of power while white people, called noughts, face systemic discrimination, the novel follows two childhood friends whose growing romantic feelings threaten to destroy their lives.

Sephy Hadley belongs to a wealthy, influential Cross family, while Callum McGregor is a nought struggling against the forces seeking to determine his future. The premise is provocative, but the book offers way more than just that. For starters, the characterization is top-notch. Neither protagonist is written as a perfect hero; Sephy often struggles with her own biases and blind spots, while Callum wrestles with growing bitterness and anger.

"The Winter Knights stick together, Quint." Part of the phenomenal Edge Chronicles fantasy series, The Winter Knights centers on Quint, a squire training at the prestigious Knights Academy in the floating city of Sanctaphrax. As an unusually harsh winter threatens the city's survival, our hero finds himself caught up in a mystery involving missing knights, political rivalries, and the increasingly desperate search for stormphrax, a remarkable substance capable of weighing down the levitating rock on which Sanctaphrax rests.

The book's greatest strength is its wonderfully bizarre fantasy setting, populated by strange creatures, eccentric scholars, and fantastical technologies that feel unlike those found in more conventional fantasy adventures. All of this is realized through Chris Riddell's brilliant illustrations. However, there's also a surprising amount of darkness beneath the whimsical surface. For instance, the story explores institutional corruption, jealousy, betrayal, and the dangers of ambition.

Four worlds and four completely different ideas of what a good life looks like. This is where you belong, what it would ask of you, and what you would get back in return.

You belong somewhere old enough that the mountains have names and somebody can still tell you who gave them. Middle-earth is for people who think the right thing is worth doing even when it will not be finished in their lifetime.

You want the sea, a crew, and absolutely nobody telling you where you are allowed to go. The Grand Line is the world for people whose main ambition in life is finding out what is over there.

You would rather be feared and useful than liked and useless. The Continent is a world where the monsters are the straightforward part of the job and the people are where it all gets complicated.

You want the myths to be real and loose in the actual world, and you want to find out at about thirteen that you were never anywhere near as ordinary as everybody kept telling you.

"All I ever wanted was for her to love me and to do something meaningful with my life." An Abundance of Katherines is generally considered John Green's worst book, though it gets a worse rap than it deserves. Fundamentally, this is an eccentric but wise coming-of-age story. It's about Colin Singleton, a former child prodigy who can solve anagrams in seconds. He's also been dumped nineteen times (he's just 17, by the way), exclusively by girls named Katherine.

Devastated after his latest breakup with Katherine XIX, Colin sets off on a spontaneous road trip from Chicago with his hilarious, Judge Judy-loving best friend, Hassan. Their aimless drive lands them in the tiny, quirky town of Gutshot, Tennessee. While staying there, Colin obsesses over inventing a mathematical theorem to predict the exact breakup date of any relationship. However, he also slowly realizes that life, friendship, and love can't be reduced to a formula.

"The entire future of western civilization depends upon me making a smiley face with this ketchup." The Recruit is the opening entry in the incredibly entertaining CHERUB series, an action-packed spy saga aimed at younger teens. It revolves around James Choke, a troubled twelve-year-old whose life falls apart following his mother's sudden death. He finds himself recruited into CHERUB, a secret British intelligence organization that trains children and teenagers to undertake dangerous undercover missions.

Although the book is primarily concerned with delivering fast-paced entertainment (including fight scenes and a little romance), author Robert Muchamore brings an unexpected degree of realism to its outrageous premise. James is frequently selfish, impulsive, and unpleasant, making him a refreshingly imperfect protagonist with a real arc. The Recruit also avoids glamorizing espionage, instead leaning into its psychological pressures and myriad ethical complications.

"The time has come to arm-wrestle some demons." This one was acclaimed on release, but you don't seem to see it come up very often in the YA conversation these days. Speak focuses on Melinda Sordino, a high school freshman who becomes a social outcast after calling the police during a summer party. Too traumatized to explain what actually happened to her at that party, Melinda retreats into near-total silence. However, her art classes gradually provide an outlet for emotions she cannot otherwise express.

What follows is an honest depiction of harm and healing, one that refuses to sugarcoat anything or offer any contrived silver linings. Plus, even in the depths of depression and isolation, the protagonist's monologue is razor-sharp and observant, as well as frequently darkly funny. In short, Speak is a modern YA classic that should be more widely read.

Avengers: Doomsday TV Spot Confirms RDJ's Doctor Doom Has A Scarred Face

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Marvel Comics fans know Doctor Victor von Doom is scarcely ever seen without his iron mask — a mask that hides his supposedly horribly scarred face. But with Marvel Studios choosing former Tony Stark actor Robert Downey Jr. to play Doom in the upcoming "Avengers" movies "Doomsday" and "Secret Wars," there's been an expectation that Doom's mask coming off will be a jaw dropping moment.

Now, Chris Evans has claimed that Doom wears his mask for the entirety of "Doomsday." But that could only mean he'll keep the mask on until "Secret Wars." Even then, there's the chance that Doom is so disfigured that his face isn't even recognizable as RDJ. A new TV spot for "Doomsday" does suggest Doom has his scarred face, while also teasing we're eventually going to see exactly what's under his mask ... but not yet.

In this TV spot, Magneto (Ian McKellen) tries to telekinetically rip off Doom's mask — it is made of metal, after all. But Doom does not restrict himself to the laws of science. There's evidently a magical seal on the mask (note there appear to be runic designs across Doom's costume), because glowing green tendrils extend out from Doom's hood and grab the mask, slamming it back onto Doom's face. A brief glimpse of Doom's face, visible through the mask's eyeholes, shows hints of pale macerated skin and scar tissue.

In the "Fantastic Four" comics, it was originally said that Doom suffered his scars during an experiment in college. A machine he built to contact the "nether world" (later revealed to be an attempt to reach his mother's damned soul) exploded in his face, leaving him scarred.

Now, Doom's co-creator Jack Kirby intended that Doom actually only had a thin facial scar — but Doom's ego can't stand even that small imperfection. Later "Fantastic Four" writer/artist John Byrne melded the two ideas. The machine explosion only slightly damaged Doom's face, but then Victor — convinced his face was already ruined — put his freshly forged mask on before it cooled, and that is why he has a horribly scarred face.

The greatest source of Doom's torment is completely self-inflicted. The exemplary 1989 "Doctor Strange and Doctor Doom: Triumph and Torment" goes with Byrne's interpretation. When Doom seeks out the wisdom of the wizard named the Aged Genghis, the sorcerer alludes to Doom's "minor disfigurement." We then see Doom's (barely scarred) face as Victor lashes out at the insult.

It's unclear what origin "Doomsday" will go with, if any of them. Marketing suggests the Marvel Cinematic Universe's Doom will be driven by a lost wife and child, not his mother. What is clear from this TV spot is that Marvel fans can expect to see Doom and Magneto — the two greatest villains of Marvel Comics — clash onscreen. Doom's wizardry here refutes the obvious conclusion that Magneto wins easily because he controls metal and Doom is concealed inside metal armor.

"He is pain, he is extinction," Magneto declares, setting up Doom's famous third person double entendre: "I am Doom!" Ian McKellen's Magneto was the first villain of the Marvel movie boom that kicked off with 2000's "X-Men." Bringing him back to face (and presumably fall to) Doom is some classic carnival barker marketing that would make Stan Lee proud.

"Avengers: Doomsday" is set for theatrical release on December 18, 2026.

Asha Bhosle and S. Janaki Remembered by Singer K.S. Chithra and Suhasini Maniratnam at IFFI’s Lata Mangeshkar Talk

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Asha Bhosle and S. Janaki, two of Indian cinema’s most celebrated playback singers, were remembered at the 57th International Film Festival of India (IFFI) in Goa by singer K.S. Chithra and actor-filmmaker Suhasini Maniratnam.

Bhosle, a two-time Grammy nominee whose name was referenced in Cornershop’s 1997 hit “Brimful of Asha,” died in April at 92. Janaki, known as the Nightingale of South India, died in July at 88. Chithra and Suhasini shared their memories of the two at the annual “Lata Mangeshkar Talk: The Voice That Echoes Forever,” named for Bhosle’s elder sister, the singer Lata Mangeshkar. Related Stories Elon Musk Criticizes Jeremy Culhane’s ‘SNL’ Impression: ‘They Need to Try Harder’ ‘SNL’ Sets Timothée Chalamet and Robert De Niro as Final Hosts of 2026

Chithra, who has recorded more than 25,000 songs in several Indian languages over a career spanning nearly five decades, spoke about the singers who shaped her musical sensibilities, among them Mangeshkar, P. Susheela and Janaki. She imitated Janaki as a child, while acknowledging that the veteran singer’s distinctive style was impossible to reproduce. Mangeshkar’s devotional songs, she said, had particularly impressed her with their emotional depth.

Suhasini recalled an episode from the making of “Iruvar” (1997), directed by her husband Mani Ratnam. Composer A.R. Rahman, an Oscar winner for “Slumdog Millionaire,” had invited Bhosle to record a song that needed to evoke the style of Tamil playback singer L.R. Eswari, but was unsure how to explain the reference to her. He asked Suhasini to help. She demonstrated Eswari’s style, and Bhosle went on to deliver “Venilla Venilla.”

Chithra spoke about Bhosle’s versatility and her ability to move between musical forms, including ghazals, and about her desire to establish her own musical identity rather than be compared with her sister. At their first meeting, Chithra said, Bhosle asked her why she was singing in such a high register.

Janaki, Suhasini said, could inhabit a character through her voice, singing like a child or a teenage boy. Chithra recalled that Janaki was a devoted fan of Mangeshkar. She would play Mangeshkar’s songs on the radio, turn down the volume during the vocal portions and record herself singing along – “like a karaoke on her own,” as Chithra described it. Offstage, Chithra said, Janaki would feed her by hand when she visited her home.

The session also touched on the demands of recording and on recognizing one’s vocal limitations with age.

Chithra sang snippets throughout, including songs from her own repertoire and numbers popularized by Janaki and Bhosle. In a closing quiz, film clips were played without their background scores and Chithra was asked to identify each song by singing it. She identified all five.

The session ended with Chithra singing “Paadariyen” from “Sindhu Bhairavi” (1985), which won her the first of her six National Film Awards, as Suhasini, who starred in the film, joined her, lip-syncing.

Jesse Plemons on the ‘Honor’ of Playing Plutarch in ‘The Hunger Games’ After Philip Seymour Hoffman and Debating Director Francis Lawrence Over the Character’s Motivations

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

“The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping” might be Haymitch Abernathy’s prequel movie, but Oscar nominee Jesse Plemons was the first person cast in the film.

Plemons plays Plutarch Heavensbee, a Capitol insider, who becomes a key figure in the rebellion that sparks in the latter half of Suzanne Collins’ original trilogy of dystopian novels. In the second book, “Catching Fire,” and its 2013 film adaptation, Plutarch is introduced as the Head Gamemaker, tasked with creating the brutal trials in the Hunger Games. (The late Philip Seymour Hoffman originated the role.) At the end of that story, Katniss Everdeen (Jennifer Lawrence) learns that Plutarch was really scheming from inside the establishment to help support the rebels. Related Stories James Talarico Crashes ‘SNL’ Cold Open: ‘I’m the Guy Who’s Been Texting You Every Day Asking for $5’ ‘SNL’ Weekend Update Brutally Roasts Elon Musk Over 'Epic’ Midterms Defeat

But “Sunrise on the Reaping” takes place 25 years before those events, so casting the younger Plutarch was key.

“I’m a huge fan of Jesse’s,” franchise director Francis Lawrence tells Variety. “Had never worked with him before, and had always wanted to, and thought he would be perfect.”

Plemons, of course, is one of the blockbuster movie’s most accomplished stars — one of the star-studded ensemble’s seven Oscar nominees: Jennifer Lawrence and Kieran Culkin are winners, with Glenn Close receiving an honorary Oscar earlier this month.

“We had a lot of fun casting all these people fans are familiar with for this movie. But with [Plutarch] specifically, there was a lot more development,” Lawrence adds, sharing that he and “Hunger Games” producer Nina Jacobson spent considerable time working with Collins on Plutarch’s arc.

“He’s an infiltrator,” Lawrence explains. “He’s the guy at the beginning of the formation of the resistance, and at the same time, has to get to the place where by ‘Catching Fire’ he’s at Snow’s side and so trusted. So, it was on us to figure out how to portray Plutarch in a way that is believable. And that was fun because we were adding scenes that weren’t in the book.”

Book readers might’ve noticed a new Plutarch scene in the “Sunrise on the Reaping” movie — where Plemons appears bruised and bloodied after being brutally interrogated by President Snow (Ralph Fiennes), who’s learned of a secret plot to sabotage the 50th Hunger Games.

“That was a keystone scene for us, and we went over and over and over different approaches of it,” Lawrence says. “How can we have him be active on these two distinct paths: one is, ‘How can I keep this resistance alive, but a secret?’ And ‘How can I seem utterly loyal and faithful to Snow, who is very, very smart?’ It’s become one of my favorite scenes in the movie.”

Lawrence kicked off conversations with Plemons by sending Collins’ unpublished manuscript. The actor cracked open the book, but because he was also working on “Digger,” he hadn’t made it to the final pages before the finished script came in.

“I read the script, and then finished the book, and was surprised by how engaging it was and how complex the character was,” Plemons tells Variety over the phone from his home in Texas. “And I was really blown away by the script and the book.”

Indeed, Plutarch is a meaty character. But he also had to consider that the role was originated by Hoffman, one of his favorite actors of all time, who’d also played his father in 2012’s “The Master.” As such, Plemons knew saying yes would come with plenty of questions from reporters about how it felt to continue the late Hoffman’s “legacy” after the actor’s unexpected death in 2014. Even after filming the part, Plemons hasn’t quite ironed out how to articulate his feelings on such a lofty question.

“That’s a tough one to answer,” Plemons replies, thoughtfully, when I ask how he feels about all of that. “I’m definitely honored to have the opportunity to play this character. I wish I had a better answer for you, but it’s complicated.”

Less complicated are his feelings about the “Hunger Games” franchise’s larger message, which was a key factor in his decision to take the part.

“I find it a big accomplishment when a film or a book that has such a wide appeal is able to tackle real-world themes and issues everyone feels and relates to, that connect to all of our lives,” Plemons says.

Read on as Plemons explains how he approached Plutarch’s prequel arc, including what he garnered from Hoffman’s performance, and why he and Lawrence debated over the complex character’s true motivations.

Many things. One, just the nature of doing a prequel like this. It was interesting knowing where the character ends up and how all these relationships progress, and then realizing there was actually more space to play and create ourselves. To fill in this very large blank over the course of 25 years. And there was something, just in the scope of the amount of time that he has been fighting and infiltrating the capital, there’s a sort of double agent aspect.

There’s a sort of philosopher side to him. There’s most definitely an importance on knowledge that he’s trying to instill in Haymitch. And at the same time, what I was most interested in was his very pragmatic sense of hope, which is not at all shrouded in any sort of rose-colored idealism. It’s very rooted in the reality of how difficult and almost impossible it’s going to be, and I was just really intrigued by that on a personal level.

The other interesting aspect is that he winds up being with the good guys, the resistance. But even still, in his relationship with Haymitch, I feel like I was constantly questioning — and I feel like the audience probably will too — just how expendable he feels Haymitch is for the greater good. All the while, he’s at least, to me, growing pretty fond of him as the story goes on. So there were just a lot of complexities that I really enjoyed.

That was the first sequence I shot, which was great. It’s obvious he’s incredibly intelligent. He’s able to sort of play this role. And I really loved that sequence with Glenn [Close]. I’m assuming they’ve known each other practically all of Plutarch’s life; she’s probably like a crazy aunt in some way. And it was really fun to play with maniuplating Drusilla without her knowing it.

It’s interesting. Obviously, his agenda is to prove himself to Snow and everyone in the Capitol. And at the same time, there must be something kind of alluring about pulling this [rebellion] off. It was really interesting, and Francis definitely allowed for some space, especially in the sequence where we’re filming Haymitch saying goodbye. We brought in a little more of the book there and a little more of Plutarch handling the camera operator, being a little more hands-on than was in the scene.

That scene, because it was a new addition that wasn’t in the book, was something that I could definitely see the need for — to see where Plutarch was in all of this. Aside from seeing in the library earlier on, it’s the only time you get to see Plutarch with Snow, and it was just an absolute thrill getting to work with Ralph.

It’s such a game of intellect between the two. Plutarch is trying to gauge how much Snow knows, whether he knows that he’s involved and who he knows is involved. At the same time, he’s trying to double down and act as if he’s truly working in the interest of the Capitol. We played around a lot with that scene.

I don’t think there’s like a black-or-white, or cut-and-dry, easy way of categorizing Plutarch or his motivations. The main debate Francis and I were in was whether or not his motivations were rooted in some sort of self-centeredness. A kind of wanting to be the hero, or just wanting to stick it to the Capitol and his family, that I’m sure, as a child, he had very conflicted feelings about.

I felt his intentions were pure. The fact that he would like to stick it to the Capitol, that’s most definitely part of it. He’s most definitely entitled and privileged. But I think everyone’s given a choice, and at some point, Plutarch decided that knowledge is of the utmost importance, and I think, morally, he is very much on the side of what is right and he sees the injustice. I believe his intentions are pure. I just don’t know why you would make it all that time if your motivations were about your own self-interest.

I had a lot of conversations with some of the other actors that were playing characters that were established in the other films, and we all had our own unique challenges and thoughts about how best to capture that essence, while at the same time taking some freedom in the fact that 24-25 years is a long time. The thing that jumped out at me in the film that he was in [Hoffman first appeared in 2013’s “Catching Fire”] was that they were still most definitely fully committed to the cause, but you could see the time. You could see the weariness of all those characters.

There were little things that I was conscious of and tried to connect to his performance, but for the most part, I felt like this is a very different Plutarch. He’s beginning his journey and is incredibly ambitious, and the whole energy of his pursuit just felt very different, and all the relationships, like his relationship with Snow, are just beginning. So, it wound up feeling like there was quite a bit of room.

Most definitely. I can’t imagine where her mind will go next — no one can. But, personally, selfishly, what I would love to see is Plutarch’s teenage years. I thought a lot about that while we were shooting, but I don’t think I could play that part.

This interview has been edited and condensed for length and clarity.

All 3 The Rings Of Power Seasons, Ranked Worst To Best

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

You'd need to go back to 2003 and The Return of the King to find the last universally acclaimed Tolkien adaptation. Whether it be Peter Jackson's The Hobbit movies and The War of the Rohirrim on the big screen, or Prime Video's The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power on TV, many years have passed since Middle-earth experienced any degree of critical consensus.

The Rings of Power has proven especially divisive. The mere concept of Amazon bringing the Second Age into live-action triggered controversy by the bucket load, and opinions split further thanks to a series of significant deviations from canon: Sauron and Galadriel's romance, the 'orcs aren't so bad' angle, and Tom Bombadil: Defense Against the Dark Arts teacher.

As much as it's difficult to shower The Rings of Power with undiluted adulation, it's also impossible to deny there are elements that J.D. Payne and Patrick McKay's series does very well. This, combined with each season covering distinct periods of the Second Age's timeline, means that The Rings of Power's three seasons released so far vary considerably in quality.

The Rings of Power season 4 is confirmed and, if the showrunners have their way, a fifth and final season will follow sometime thereafter. Until then, The Rings of Power is following a very distinct trend in terms of overall quality.

The Rings of Power didn't exactly put its best foot forward at the beginning. It seems strange now, but the series initially thought the best way to adapt source material almost a century old was by following a 'mystery box' format. The Rings of Power's first season banked hard on questions like, "Who is the Stranger?" and "Who is Halbrand?" Answers to these plot points were handled like huge revelations, but in reality, the actor who looked like a young Gandalf turned out to be playing Gandalf, and the character behaving like Sauron turned out to be Sauron.

Looking back, season 1 kept its cards too close to its chest, and the whole affair ended up more akin to a Lord of the Rings imitator than a genuine Second Age TV series. The Stranger stood in for Gandalf, Adar was black metal's answer to Sauron, Nori and Poppy were your new Frodo and Sam, and Arondir covered the same bases as Legolas. Were it not for the folks in Lindon and Númenor, viewers would have been forgiven for asking whether The Rings of Power was actually a licensed Middle-earth production.

The focus on ambiguity and misdirection took the place of a compelling storyline. Not only does The Rings of Power's first season bear the least resemblance to Tolkien, it's the one season where little of note happens. The Harfoots aren't as interesting as The Rings of Power wants them to be (remember Lenny Henry was a main cast member?), Gil-galad is a shadow of himself, and Númenor is still enjoying some calm before the storm.

It's not all bad, of course. Mordor gets a genuinely fascinating origin story as the Southlands transforms into the volcanic realm The Lord of the Rings fans know and love, while Keeping Up with Khazad-dûm offers some much-needed light relief among all the wistful glances and overwrought foreshadowing.

With Sauron slipping into his Annatar guise, The Rings of Power finally found what it needed most: a canon cornerstone to build from. Wisely spending much time in Eregion, The Rings of Power's second chapter spanned the creation of the Rings of Power themselves, then ended on the Siege of Eregion, one of the Second Age's most memorable battles.

The improvement was palpable. The entire angle hinged on the chemistry between Charlie Vickers' Sauron and Charles Edwards' Celebrimbor, as the pair entered a marriage of blood, fire, and metal. The Rings of Power perfectly captured the nuances of that relationship: Celebrimbor's lofty ambition, Sauron's silky manipulations, and the authentic respect forged between them as they toiled. It also helped that, after an entire first season largely without rings, The Rings of Power finally lived up to its name in season 2, adapting a recognizable story from Tolkien's lore and being more or less faithful.

Tom Bombadil randomly popping up in Rhûn was odd. You'd almost call it fan service, but without the version of Tom fans would know from the books. Galadriel's pining over Sauron also started to grate very quickly, especially after Elrond stepped into the love triangle via a misjudged kiss. And the finale still dropped the 'Gandalf' reveal like the audience still hadn't realized Daniel Weyman wasn't just playing some random wizard.

Generally speaking, however, season 2 was where The Rings of Power started to justify its existence. There was a balance between hard-hitting battle scenes and intelligent character drama, and much less effort spent on trying to keep Tolkien readers on their toes with guessing games. Númenor was a particular highlight, as the tension between Míriel and her bearded cousin boiled over into some rather excellent political backstabbing.

One of the biggest compliments that can be paid to The Rings of Power is that it's still getting better three seasons in. After season 2 ended with the Siege of Eregion, it was left for The Rings of Power's third season to explore the War of Sauron and the Elves, while also handling the Faithful vs. King's Men civil war over in Númenor. The result was Amazon's best season of expensive Middle-earth content yet and, perhaps not coincidentally, the truest to the source material.

By some distance, Sauron shone brightest. Charlie Vickers spent the entire season attempting to construct the One Ring, and his performance exemplified the physical and emotional strain of crafting Arda's most powerful artifact this side of the Silmarils. What Tolkien described of the Ring's creation would fit onto an envelope, and The Rings of Power did admirably to stay within those lines, while also adding logical color between them. It made perfect sense that Sauron needed to know more about the Three before he could control them. And the One Ring's rhythmic method of construction went right back to the biblical opening of The Silmarillion.

The Númenor material continued the good work of season 2, depicting the Faithful as an underground rebellion, and Gil-galad finally turned into a character who vaguely resembles what Tolkien created. One can question whether renaming the Nazgûl was strictly necessary, but watching the likes of Khamûl, Theo, and the two fallen kings in the early stages of their corruption proved an absolute treat. To the east, Gandalf improved immeasurably once his arc dovetailed into the main happenings in Middle-earth.

The Rings of Power isn't quite there in terms of winning over skeptics just yet. Durin's 'wife slept with brother' storyline felt a little too soap opera-y, with very real fears of Disa shouting, "We were on a break!" at one point. The coronation of Thrain contained just a little too much Coronation Street for a Middle-earth series.

It would also be fair to argue that, for a season revolving entirely around two massive wars, The Rings of Power season 3 contained very little war. Opening battle aside, you'd never know conflict was even afoot. Indeed, The Rings of Power twice pulled back from battle scenes at the last minute: once when news of Míriel's wedding prevented the Faithful charging against the King's Men, and then again when Sauron's surrender avoided a clash between his forces and the Númenor-Elf alliance. Hopefully the battle budget is being saved for season 5.

Nevertheless, The Rings of Power sails toward its fourth season with the critical winds at its back, and the gradual improvement seen since the beginning offers reason for optimism that Sauron's trip to Númenor will not disappoint.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

‘Stranger Things’ Star's ‘Hellboy’ Reboot Officially Leaves Netflix in 5 Days

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Mike Mignola's monster-hunting demon Hellboy has graced the big screen four times now. The most controversial take on the character can currently be found on Netflix, but you'd better act fast, or it'll be dragged to Hell. David Harbour donned the crimson prosthetics to portray the character, presaging his current success in the Marvel Cinematic Universe.

Mignola created Hellboy in 1994 as a creator-owned character; he has gone on to become one of the greatest horror comic successes of the modern era, even after Mignola concluded his story in Hellboy in Hell. The character first graced the screen in 2004 in Guillermo del Toro's Hellboy: Ron Perlman played the title character with his customary aplomb, while Doug Jones played his aquatic ally Abe Sapien, presaging his amphibious star turn in The Shape of Water, and Selma Blair starred as Hellboy's pyrokinetic ex Liz Sherman. The trio reprised their roles in 2008's Hellboy II: The Golden Army; a third film was mooted, but del Toro moved on and Perlman was unwilling to make the film without him, and so the franchise was rebooted.

2019's Hellboy stars Stranger Things' Harbour as the title character, a demon who's been raised in the bosom of the Bureau for Paranormal Research and Defense by Professor Trevor Bruttenholm (Ian McShane, Deadwood). He teams with BPRD were-jaguar Ben Daimio (Daniel Dae Kim, Lost) and medium Alice Monaghan (Sasha Lane, Twisters) in a race against time as the porcine fairy Gruagach (Stephen Graham, Adolescence) attempts to reassemble the dismembered body of the sorceress Vivienne Nimue (Milla Jovovich, Resident Evil). Can they stop her resurrection before she brings about the End Times? You've got until November 30, when Hellboy leaves Netflix, to find out.

Four worlds and four completely different ideas of what a good life looks like. This is where you belong, what it would ask of you, and what you would get back in return.

You belong somewhere old enough that the mountains have names and somebody can still tell you who gave them. Middle-earth is for people who think the right thing is worth doing even when it will not be finished in their lifetime.

You want the sea, a crew, and absolutely nobody telling you where you are allowed to go. The Grand Line is the world for people whose main ambition in life is finding out what is over there.

You would rather be feared and useful than liked and useless. The Continent is a world where the monsters are the straightforward part of the job and the people are where it all gets complicated.

You want the myths to be real and loose in the actual world, and you want to find out at about thirteen that you were never anywhere near as ordinary as everybody kept telling you.

Hellboy was not the success the studio was hoping for with critics, fans, or audiences. The next iteration of the franchise took shape five years later, with Hellboy: The Crooked Man. An adaptation of a miniseries from Mignola and late horror comics legend Richard Corben, The Crooked Man took a stripped-down approach to the material, with a low budget and a largely unknown cast: Jack Kesy (Deadpool 2) stars as Hellboy, who finds himself in the backwoods of Appalachia as he hunts a monstrous creature. It was released in theaters internationally and direct-to-VOD in the US in 2024.

Hellboy is leaving Netflix on November 30. Stay tuned to Collider for future updates.

Gerald R. Molen, Schindler’s List & Jurassic Park Producer, Dies at 91

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Academy Award-winning producer Gerald R. Molen has passed away.

Molen, the iconic producer behind several of Steven Spielberg’s most critically acclaimed movies, has sadly died at the age of 91. Throughout his impressive career in the entertainment industry, Molen helped bring some of the industry's most famous films to the big screen, such as Jurassic Park, Schindler’s List, and Minority Report.

According to The Hollywood Reporter, Molen died on Monday, November 23, 2026. The producer's great-niece, Rachel Lorraine Pullan, confirmed the news on social media. The cause of death has not been disclosed at the time of this article's publication.

Molen's professional relationship with Spielberg began when he served as the unit production manager on the director’s 1985 drama The Color Purple. His work on the project opened many doors for the producer, leading him into a whole new chapter of his career. Spielberg also offered him an associate producer position on the 1987 science fiction comedy Batteries Not Included.

They went on to collaborate on many movies, such as 1991’s Hook, starring Robin Williams, Dustin Hoffman, and Julia Roberts. Molen eventually became the head of production at Spielberg’s Amblin Entertainment, where he managed the productions over several of the studio's projects.

During his time at Amblin, Molen was in charge of numerous major releases throughout the 1990s, including The Flintstones, The Little Rascals, Little Giants, Casper, and Twister. He was also involved with Spielberg’s 1997 dinosaur blockbuster The Lost World: Jurassic Park.

Every Harry Potter Book, Ranked by Goodreads

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The Harry Potter franchise is a legendary series in the world of children's literature. The series defined an entire generation of readers with its inventive wizarding world and how it matured alongside the audience, dealing with heavy themes of death, love, and corruption born of systemic prejudices. Warner Bros. adapted the books into an equally successful film series, which brought the franchise to a wider audience and has served as the baseline for future additions, such as the Fantastic Beasts prequels.

Though the last book came out in 2007, the Harry Potter saga has maintained a dedicated fanbase and continues to delight and entrap new readers. Everyone has their preferences, but the consensus tends to look more positively towards some books compared to others. Fans on Goodreads have ranked each Potter book, and while the lowest-rated might be predictable, the highest one might be a bit unsurprising.

Book two follows Harry's second year at Hogwarts School of Witchcraft and Wizardry, though it doesn't start off very well. A House Elf named Dobby tries to prevent him from going on account of a strange plot, so Harry and his friend Ronald Weasley have to fly an enchanted car to get to school. However, as the semester goes on, students are attacked by a mysterious creature that is linked to Salazar Slytherin, one of the school's four founders.

Harry Potter and the Chamber of Secrets is not a bad book, but consensus does put it as the worst in the main series. Examples of why include a repetitive structure to its predecessor, Hogwarts' staff looking incompetent as the attacks escalate, a number of plot contrivances and deus ex machina, and the fact that Albus Dumbledore would hire such an unqualified teacher as Gilderoy Lockhart to teach Defense Against the Dark Arts. That said, it has its positives as well, such as the introduction of Dobby, memorable set pieces like the Womping Willow and the titular chamber, and an expansion of the Wizarding World through new spells, monsters, and tension between pure-blood wizards and those of mixed heritage.

The one that started it all. Harry Potter and the Philosopher's Stone introduced the world to Harry Potter, a young boy abused by his spiteful and selfish aunt and uncle, who learns on his 11th birthday that he is a wizard. Upon entering the Wizarding World, Harry also learns that he is a celebrity because, as an infant, he survived the Dark Lord, Voldemort, and somehow brought about his downfall. Unfortunately, while he does make new friends upon entering Hogwarts, his school year is interrupted by various strange happenings linked to an item stored in the castle.

The book perfectly captures the beautiful and whimsical nature of the Wizarding World, making you just as eager as Harry to learn more about it. Rowling also does a good job of crafting an effective mystery around who is trying to get the titular stone and why. Alas, due to being the first book in the series, it does suffer from the need to establish the world and principal characters, and the plot does have a few holes/contrivances, but the rest of the series exists thanks to its foundations.

Harry's fifth year at Hogwarts is a doozy: the Ministry of Magic refuses to believe that Lord Voldemort has returned, instead thinking this is a plot by Dumbledore to seize power. Thus, Dolores Umbridge is sent to Hogwarts to keep an eye on things and ensure that Dumbledore can't use the students as his personal army. Meanwhile, Harry is struggling to process his trauma from Voldemort's return, while the Dark Lord's minions hunt for a prophecy that holds the details of his defeat.

Harry Potter and the Order of the Phoenix is the longest book in the series and is filled with lots of good and bad. The worst part is Harry's grief, which, while presented realistically, does make him frustrating to follow because of how short-tempered and aggravating he is. Fortunately, it's balanced out by Umbridge's unique brand of villainy, great backstory revelations that reveal more about the First Wizarding War, and a fantastic climax featuring rooms in the Department of Mysteries and a creative duel between Dumbledore and Voldemort.

Harry's fourth year at Hogwarts sees it host the Triwizard Tournament, a friendship-building event with two sister schools: Beauxbatons Academy of Magic and Durmstrang Institute. However, everyone is shocked when the magical Goblet of Fire that chooses the participants reveals Harry's name as well, even though he never put his name in. Due to the binding magic of the goblet, Harry has no choice but to compete, forcing him to prepare for three deadly challenges while trying to solve the mystery of how and why his name showed up in the first place.

Harry Potter and the Goblet of Fire is officially when the series shifts from the whimsical adventures in magic schools to the much darker and more complex stories that dominate its later half. The tournament itself offers plenty of exciting set pieces, from fire-breathing dragons to a hedge maze full of monsters. Then, while waiting for the next challenge, the characters partake in all manner of subplots, including relationship drama, missing gold, a reporter who knows way more than she should, and Hermione's attempt to give the House Elves equal rights.

Harry's sixth year takes place during the Second Wizarding War, casting a net of uncertainty as he wonders about Voldemort's next move and how to stop him. To that end, Dumbledore takes Harry through a series of memories to decipher clues about Voldemort's powers, and hopes that Harry can get the last one they need from Professor Horace Slughorn. Meanwhile, Harry also starts keeping an eye on Draco Malfoy, whom he believes has become a Death Eater and is planning something nefarious.

Harry Potter and the Half-Blood Prince is the book that sets up everything we need to know for the endgame of the series. We get a massive deep dive into Voldemort's backstory that helps explain how he became the Dark Lord we know today; the mysteries are engaging, and the ending is one of the most shocking moments in children's literature. That said, it's also the book where Rowling tried to describe Harry's growing feelings for Ginny Weasley as a green monster roaring in his chest, and it's got a lot of awkward couple drama, which can be a bit of a turnoff.

Harry's third year of Hogwarts almost ends before it begins when he accidentally casts a spell that turns his aunt Marge into a balloon and runs away from home. While he's still allowed to go back to school, he's informed that a former servant of Voldemort's named Sirius Black has escaped from Azkaban Prison, and the horrific emotion-sucking Dementors that guard the prison are out hunting for him. Fortunately, the new Defense Against the Dark Arts Teacher, Remus Lupin, is an old friend of Harry's parents and offers to help him learn a spell to keep the Dementors at bay.

Harry Potter and the Prisoner of Azkaban is the first book that really takes a dark turn, but it still has tons of whimsical moments to balance them out. The dramatic moments are where the book shines, and a lot of time is dedicated to exploring concepts of good, evil, and how people change for better or for worse when pushed to their limits. It's also unique in that Voldemort has the least impact on this book, not appearing physically and hardly even being mentioned. Instead, it gives us the horrific manifestations of depression that are the Dementors as antagonists.

Rather than attend Hogwarts for their seventh year, Harry, Ron, and Hermione decide to hunt down Voldemort's Horcruxes in a bid to make him mortal. Unfortunately, they don't know where to find them, and Voldemort has taken over the Ministry of Magic, meaning that there is now a nationwide manhunt for Harry. What's more, Harry learns some shocking news about the early life of Albus Dumbledore, making him question if he can truly trust his old professor or not.

Harry Potter and the Deathly Hallows had the monumental task of bringing this series to a close, and it's safe to say that it struck the landing. The first half of the book is the hunt for the Horcruxes, while the second half is structured around the Battle of Hogwarts, where the heroes face off against Voldemort's army of dark wizards, werewolves, giants, Dementors, and other foul creatures. Amongst it all are some fantastic explorations of legacy, death, sacrifice, and presenting long-established characters as more complex than they first appeared.
