Solo Leveling's Next Big Release Just Got A Game-Changing Update

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Merlyn De Souza is a freelance writer and anime enthusiast based in Goa, India, with a Bachelor's in English Literature. When she isn't writing, she can be found exploring various realms of entertainment, including, but not limited to, K-dramas, novels, and even the occasional manhwa. She also enjoys all things creative, like sewing and makeup.

After a quiet couple of years, Solo Leveling is steadily building up to a massive return across various media and adaptations in 2027 and 2028, whether that's the anime, the movie, the multi-platform RPG, or its promising Netflix live-action series. From its humble origins as a web novel to its journey into one of the most popular action fantasy webtoons out there, to now a sensational anime series, Solo Leveling's franchise only continues to level up, much like Jinwoo, with every new addition.

As such, hopes are high even for its upcoming live-action adaptation helmed by Netflix and Sanai Pictures, which, according to recent reports, is aiming for a 2028 release. Production seemed to be going smoothly so far, with filming having kicked off back in May 2026. That said, six months into filming, Solo Leveling's live-action just hit what could be a major roadblock as the series faces its biggest production shake-up yet.

Not even halfway into production, Netflix's Solo Leveling live-action series has abruptly changed hands with the entry of a new director after one of the original directors dropped out of the series. According to a report by OSEN on November 25, director Lee Hae-jun has stepped down from the Solo Leveling live-action after "extensive talks" with Netflix, Sanai Pictures, and Kakao Entertainment, with Han Dong-wook set to take his place. Han Dong-wook has most notably previously directed the hit 2023 thriller, The Worst of Evil.

A new Netflix show is coming that, if it combines the elements of Jujutsu Kaisen and Ready Player One that it shares, could prove better than both.

Solo Leveling's live-action series was originally set to be co-directed by Lee Hae-jun and Kim Byung-seo. According to the report by OSEN, the talks about Lee Hae-jun's departure ended with an amicable agreement, though the exact reason why he decided to drop out of the project is still unknown.

All that said, this sudden change has indeed raised many concerns about the live-action series and its fate, some of which were addressed by the Solo Leveling production team. Expanding a bit on the change, the Solo Leveling team told Newsen that director Lee Hae-jun had directed up to Episode 2 of Solo Leveling's reported seven-episode run, and that the goal was to have different directors in charge of different episodes, with Han Dong-wook and Kim Byung-seo set to take up the remaining episodes.

"It is similar to existing series where different directors direct different episodes. Director Lee Hae-jun has finished filming up to Episode 2, and directors Han Dong-wook and Kim Byung-seo are scheduled to continue filming the remaining episodes."

It is unclear whether the remaining five episodes will be co-directed or split between the two directors, and how exactly they will be split if so. This format of having different directors in charge of separate episodes also raises concerns about inconsistent and possibly conflicting visions across episodes. The new director, Han Dong-wook, is undoubtedly brilliant. However, it is also very apparent that this production model was not always the plan from the start, and it could very well be what dooms Solo Leveling's live-action, which until now seemed to be a very promising adaptation.

Solo Leveling follows Sung Jin-woo, the world's weakest hunter, who gains extraordinary powers through a mysterious program after surviving a brutal monster attack. As he navigates his newfound abilities, Jinwoo embarks on a quest to uncover the secrets of his powers and the dungeon that altered his fate.

‘Hunger Games’ Producer Regrets Splitting ‘Mockingjay’ As Fans Felt It Was A “Play To Make Them See Two Films Instead Of One”

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

With the release of the prequel The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping, franchise producer Nina Jacobson regrets splitting the Mockingjay films into two parts.

At the time, The Twilight Saga and Harry Potter split their grand finale into two films, and Lionsgate followed suit, breaking up the last Suzanne Collins book of the OG Hunger Games trilogy into two films as well.

“Even though I love the Mockingjay films, if I had it to do over again, I think both Francis [Lawrence, director] and I might try to do it all in one,” she said in an interview with Empire.

She continued, “Mockingjay was such a big book, and it takes you to so many different places. But in the end, I think that Francis and I both felt that splitting them up… even if it did come from an inside-out place of really feeling like we needed two films to tell the story, I think audiences find it cynical. Even if its intentions are earnest, which they were. They feel like it’s a play to make them see two films instead of one.” Watch on Deadline

With Collins’ two prequels like The Hunger Games: The Ballad of Songbirds and Snakes and Sunrise on the Reaping, producers have changed the way they adapt the books.

“We really put the burden on ourselves [now], even when it’s a big book, to transform it into a theatrical experience, to not take advantage of your audience’s patience, to make it as tight as we can,” Jacobson told the publication. “[Sunrise On The Reaping] is the longest by a minute or two of all of them.”

The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping sees Jennifer Lawrence, Josh Hutcherson, and Woody Harrelson return to their roles from the original trilogy. The film stars Joseph Zada playing a young Haymitch, and is joined by Mckenna Grace, Whitney Peak, Glenn Close (Drusilla), Ralph Finneas (President Snow), Billy Porter (Magno), Maya Hawke (Wiress), Elle Fanning (Effie), Percy Daggs IV (Ampert), Whitney Peak (Lenore Dove), Lili Taylor (Mags), Kiran Culkin (Caesar Flickerman), Kelvin Harrison Jr (Beetee), Jesse Plemons (Plutarch), Ben Wang (Wyatt), among others.

Star Trek: Voyager Officially Revisits the Series' Best Two-Parter in a Brand-New Way

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

One of the best two-episode arcs in Star Trek: Voyager, if not the entire Star Trek franchise, is the "Year of Hell" duology. The story from the show's fourth season pitted the crew of Voyager against a unique threat that tested their perseverance more than any previous enemy had.

Now, new DLC for survival game Star Trek: Voyager - Across the Unknown​​​​​​​ will revisit the iconic storyline. Players won't just be able to relive "Year of Hell," however, they'll be able to change the course of history.​​​​​​​

Daedalic Entertainment has announced that the fourth Expansion Pass DLC for Across the Unknown will be inspired by "Year of Hell". The announcement describes the DLC as "a self-contained mini-campaign of three new sectors." The DLC will be released on Dec. 9. As in the show​​​​​​​, the game will pit players against the Krenim captain Annorax as he wields a weapon that allows him to alter history and destroy entire species. As a standalone expansion, Year of Hell won't affect any of players' main-game save files​​​​​​​.

Unlike the base game, which sees the Voyager trying to rebuild​​​​​​​ and survive with few resources, Year of Hell will invert the traditional gameplay experience. Players will start with a fully repaired and fully equipped ship​​​​​​​ before encountering the Krenim. While damaged rooms will be repairable, lost decks will cause players to lose every room, and players can lose up to five decks​​​​​​​. Crew members can also die, leaving the Voyager crew scrambling to stay alive​​​​​​​. Daedalic Entertainment says that the goal of the DLC "isn't to finish with a perfect ship. It's to see how many of your crew are still standing after twelve months."

If players want to follow the plot of the "Year of Hell" episodes, that is absolutely possible, but Daedalic​​​​​​​ has given players another option. While Captain Janeway can make alliances with a number of friendly ships, she can also choose to join Annorax. "Help Annorax repair the timeline, once he agrees to stop erasing civilizations. Whether he keeps that promise is another matter. Your senior officers may not share your enthusiasm, and their disapproval grows as the months pass."

Depending on which path players take​​​​​​​, the other inhabitants of the sectors will have shifting opinions of the Voyager and act accordingly. The base game is required, but those who own the Expansion Pass will automatically receive Year of Hell when it is released. In the meantime, you can rewatch the "Year of Hell" episodes​​​​​​​ to prepare for your own fight against Annorax.

LAIKA Officially Says Goodbye to Hulu in 4 Days

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Hannah covers horror, sci-fi, and games at Collider, where she writes news and features, reviews new releases, and interviews the people who make them. She’s helping build out Collider’s gaming coverage, with Pokémon as one of her specialties. If she isn’t writing about a monster, she’s probably watching one on a tape from her VHS collection.

For an animated movie with a PG rating, Coraline has definitely been responsible for more than its fair share of childhood nightmares. Henry Selick, who had already given an entire generation a reason to associate Christmas with Halloween with The Nightmare Before Christmas, found something genuinely terrifying in the prospect of having parents who gave their child everything she wanted. Of course, everything comes at a cost in the Other World, and Teri Hatcher’s Other Mother makes the prospect of sewing buttons over your eyes sound disturbingly reasonable.

Seventeen years later, Coraline is arguably more popular than ever. LAIKA’s decision to bring the movie back to theaters in remastered 3D for its 15th anniversary in 2024 proved incredibly lucrative, generating $38.3 million worldwide within its first two weekends alone. With the studio finally returning to feature movies this year with Wildwood, several of its earlier stop-motion adventures are about to undergo a change in streaming availability.

Coraline is scheduled to leave Hulu on December 1, with November 30 marking the final day to watch the movie on the platform. Fortunately, LAIKA fans can still find the movie on HBO Max, where it is available in 4K. The departure also affects two other Oscar-nominated movies from the animation studio, making this Thanksgiving weekend an excellent opportunity for a LAIKA marathon on Hulu.

Four worlds and four completely different ideas of what a good life looks like. This is where you belong, what it would ask of you, and what you would get back in return.

You belong somewhere old enough that the mountains have names and somebody can still tell you who gave them. Middle-earth is for people who think the right thing is worth doing even when it will not be finished in their lifetime.

You want the sea, a crew, and absolutely nobody telling you where you are allowed to go. The Grand Line is the world for people whose main ambition in life is finding out what is over there.

You would rather be feared and useful than liked and useless. The Continent is a world where the monsters are the straightforward part of the job and the people are where it all gets complicated.

You want the myths to be real and loose in the actual world, and you want to find out at about thirteen that you were never anywhere near as ordinary as everybody kept telling you.

Both ParaNorman (2012) and Kubo and the Two Strings (2016) are scheduled to leave Hulu alongside Coraline. The three movies represent a hugely significant period in LAIKA’s history, with each receiving an Academy Award nomination for Best Animated Feature. Kubo and the Two Strings director Travis Knight also received a second nomination for the movie's visual effects alongside his fellow nominees, a remarkable achievement for a stop-motion production. All three movies are currently available on HBO Max.

Meanwhile, Knight returned to the Pacific Northwest for LAIKA's sixth feature film, Wildwood, which opened in theaters on October 23. Adapted from Colin Meloy and Carson Ellis’s illustrated fantasy adventure, the movie follows a young girl who ventures into a mysterious forest after her baby brother is abducted by crows. As of November 22, the movie has grossed more than $52 million domestically. With Wildwood, LAIKA has once again entrusted a remarkably dark fantasy adventure to a young heroine navigating an unfamiliar world.

Coraline is streaming on Hulu through November 30 and remains available on HBO Max.

8 Modern Thriller Books To Read Before Kicking the Bucket

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

There's plenty of adrenaline to be found in modern thriller books. With anxiety, crime, and social tensions seemingly at an all-time high, these novels keep readers up past midnight, tapping into some of society's biggest fears. Whether it’s fractured relationships or institutional corruption, thriller books translate these tensions into pages.

Whether you're into psychological mind games or constant plot twists, there's something for every kind of thriller fan. From an international murder conspiracy to a desperate attempt to prove someone's loyalty to both sides of a war, here are modern thriller books to read before kicking the bucket.

Shutter Island is proof that books can be better than their movie adaptations. Dennis Lehane's novel follows U.S. Marshal Teddy Daniels and his new partner, Chuck Aule, who are sent to a remote island to investigate a patient's disappearance from a hospital for the criminally insane. The staff's strange behavior makes their investigation anything but routine, and a storm soon leaves them with no way off the island.

Unlike the movie, the novel allows Teddy's descent into madness to progress more organically. The limited point of view makes his increasingly questionable thoughts and assumptions feel convincing, and that's all thanks to Lehane's ability to hide Teddy's real experiences with eloquent prose. With the hospital staff constantly making him question his own judgment, readers are left just as uncertain as Teddy about what is real and what isn't.

Viet Thanh Nguyen's The Sympathizer explores the Vietnam War through the eyes of someone living in a constant state of in-betweens. A half-Vietnamese, half-French communist spy, the novel's unnamed narrator is forced to flee Saigon for Los Angeles. Despite having to rebuild his life in a new country, he secretly keeps tabs on South Vietnamese military officials living in exile.

It's a never-ending losing game in The Sympathizer, with the narrator unable to please either side without betraying the other. He is constantly caught between his political beliefs, personal identity, and the need to stay alive. No matter where he goes, he cannot simply leave his past behind. The Sympathizer explores this constant duality between two identities, two loyalties, and two countries, showing just how difficult it is to start over when his own allegiances keep pulling him in opposite directions.

BookTok sensations do not always deserve their stellar reputation, but Alex Michaelides' The Silent Patient is a cautionary tale of what it means to be a silenced woman, quite literally. Alicia Berenson seems to have the perfect life as a successful painter married to Gabriel, until she shoots him five times. After his death, she stops speaking and is admitted to a psychiatric facility. Six years later, psychotherapist Theo Faber takes on her case.

Michaelides complicates the story by bringing in two near-convincing perspectives. Theo's investigation and Alicia's diary entries reveal different sides of the events surrounding Gabriel's murder, though neither account can be trusted entirely. With nobody telling the whole truth, readers must decide whose version to believe: the accused murderer, or a man convinced he has her figured out.

Lucy Foley's The Guest List is essentially Agatha Christie meets Knives Out. Magazine publisher Jules Keegan and television personality Will Slater gather their handpicked guests for a glamorous wedding on a remote Irish island. But the celebrations take a grim turn when a storm cuts off the island from the mainland. Before the couple can even say their "I dos," Will is found stabbed to death, leaving everyone a potential suspect.

Unlike most murder mysteries, The Guest List doesn't exactly make readers feel sorry for the victim. The guests' motives trace back to an incident from years earlier, one that could ruin Will's reputation (before he died). There's something almost satisfyingly karmic about his tragic fate. But then again, no one wants to be on the same stranded island as an unknown killer.

Toni Collette and Kit Harington are shooting a new Miss Marple and 20th Century has several more Christies in development, so the drawing room is busier than it has been in decades. Here is which of them you are, and the case for yours being the best.

Order, method, and a quiet confidence that you are the cleverest person present. Poirot is a retired Belgian police officer who solves murders by sitting still and thinking, and who is vain enough to make the reveal a performance every single time.

People tell you things because they cannot imagine you being a threat. Marple solves murders by comparing everybody to somebody she once knew in St Mary Mead, which sounds like a joke until you notice it works every time, because human beings are not as original as they think.

You have far too many ideas and roughly a fifth of them are excellent. Ariadne Oliver is a successful crime novelist who is sick of her famous foreign detective and keeps saying so, which is Christie writing about Christie and getting away with it completely.

You are steady, decent and the reason the difficult genius has anybody at all. Hastings narrates the early Poirot novels, misses most of the clues, and is the only character in the books that Poirot is unambiguously fond of, which tells you what he is really for.

Anyone who enjoyed Stephen King's Misery should give Taylor Adams' novel a read. No Exit follows college student Darby Thorne, who gets stranded at an isolated highway rest stop during a Colorado blizzard. With four strangers for company, Darby is horrified to discover a kidnapped nine-year-old girl locked inside a van outside. The worst part is that the kidnapper is one of the four strangers sharing the rest stop with her.

Darby is just someone who happens to be in the wrong place at the wrong time, which makes her situation all the more dire. With no one she can trust, she has to rely on her limited abilities to stay alive and save the child. It's a nerve-wracking survival thriller, but also a disturbing reminder that situations like these aren't just fictional scenarios and can happen to anyone, particularly women who find themselves in vulnerable situations.

Stieg Larsson's The Girl with the Dragon Tattoo follows disgraced journalist Mikael Blomkvist, who accepts a job from wealthy businessman Henrik Vanger to investigate the disappearance of his niece, Harriet, who vanished during a family gathering on the Vanger family's remote island four decades earlier. Working alongside hacker Lisbeth Salander, Mikael soon realizes that there's much more to the case.

Often considered a quintessential Nordic noir, The Girl with the Dragon Tattoo is dark, bleak, and shows little mercy towards its flawed characters. Mikael and Lisbeth are far from conventional heroes, and neither has had an easy life. In many ways, they're both just trying to survive. But as they uncover the truth behind Harriet's disappearance and the dangers surrounding it, they find a new purpose: fighting back, even if it means breaking the law.

Adapted into an HBO limited series, Sharp Objects follows journalist Camille Preaker, who reluctantly returns to her Missouri hometown to investigate the murder of a young girl and the disappearance of another. Forced to live with her estranged mother, controlling stepfather, and rebellious half-sister Amma, Camille must also confront memories of her late sister Marian. As the investigation takes a darker turn, Camille begins to suspect that the case is closer to home than she would like.

Gillian Flynn might be best known for her work Gone Girl, but Sharp Objects raises an important warning about the dangers of unaddressed intergenerational trauma. Through Camille's strained family relationships, the series explores how abuse doesn't just affect one person; it creates a hostile environment where trauma gets passed down, damages relationships, and breeds new forms of violence.

Few works of art are as iconic as Leonardo da Vinci's Mona Lisa, so it's no surprise that Dan Brown uses it as the starting point for The Da Vinci Code. After Louvre curator Jacques Saunière is found murdered, Harvard professor Robert Langdon becomes involved in an investigation that takes him through hidden messages, famous paintings, and the mystery of the Holy Grail.

Brown isn't simply interested in telling a murder mystery. He also plays with historical accounts, particularly those surrounding Christianity, by exploring what might happen if widely accepted beliefs were challenged. Some readers may find this approach uncomfortable, but it makes the novel all the more interesting. Brown takes complicated subjects like art, religion, and ancient history and turns them into a puzzle that makes readers want to crack open a history book for fun.

Robert De Niro's Mob Thriller Officially Leaves Netflix in 4 Days

Robert De Niro is back at the box office this Thanksgiving weekend, as one of his most popular comedy franchises returns for the first time in 16 years. Focker-in-Law, the fourth film in the Fockers franchise, returns audiences to De Niro's Jack Byrnes as a new face seeks a place in the circle of trust. That face is the very recognizable Ariana Grande, who portrays Olivia Jones, a former FBI hostage negotiator and girlfriend of Greg's (Ben Stiller) son Henry (Skyler Gisondo).

Owen Wilson, Teri Polo, Blythe Danner, and Beanie Feldstein also feature in this latest comedy, which is hoping to battle the likes of The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping at the box office this weekend. The underrated crime thriller The Family, which also stars Michelle Pfeiffer, Dianna Agron, and Tommy Lee Jones, is being removed from Netflix on December 1​​​​​​. Directed by Luc Besson, the film follows De Niro's Giovanni Manzoni, who rats out the mob and leaves his family with no choice but to enter the Witness Protection Program. However, as old habits die hard, danger lurks. The film was met with mixed-to-poor reviews upon release, but isn't without its fans.

Four criminal masterminds. One of them was always going to be yours. Here is who comes for you, why they picked you out of everyone available, and what they want from the encounter.

Your nemesis does not want to beat you. He wants to know you completely, and he is patient enough to wait for it. The frightening part is how good the conversation is.

Your nemesis is not after money, territory or revenge. He is after a demonstration, and you are the person he has chosen to prove his point on.

Your nemesis respects you, which sounds flattering until you work out what it means. He chose you because nobody else was worth his time.

Your nemesis has no personal interest in you whatsoever, and that is precisely what makes him unsurvivable. You are not a rival. You are a stop on the way.

Although it faced backlash from critics, De Niro starring in a gangster movie proved too enticing for theatergoers, especially considering his reputation as one of the faces of the genre. Against a reported budget of $30 million, The Family returned a respectable $80 million worldwide, split between $37 million in domestic revenue and a further $43 million from overseas markets. The Family made a further $14.5 million in DVD and Blu-ray sales.

Robert De Niro's The Family is being removed from Netflix on December 1. Make sure to stay tuned to Collider for all the latest streaming stories.

The Further Mis-Adventures Of Cliff Booth Doubles Down On A Once Upon A Time In Hollywood Controversy

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Warning: This article contains spoilers for "The Further Mis-Adventures of Cliff Booth."

It simply wouldn't be a Quentin Tarantino movie if it didn't double down on itself. After wowing audiences everywhere in 2019 with "Once Upon a Time in Hollywood," the quirky and uber-talented filmmaker returned to the scene of the crime (so to speak) once more with his long-awaited sequel/spin-off film, "The Further Mis-Adventures of Cliff Booth." This time, the buddy comedy act with Leonardo DiCaprio's Rick Dalton and Brad Pitt's Cliff has been dropped, focusing solely on the latter as the stuntman embarks on his latest round of mischief. Though Tarantino stepped aside from his directing duties to make room for David Fincher, well, let's just say that the script is as purely QT as it gets.

That quickly becomes evident as we're dropped back into this alternate-history world of 1970s Los Angeles, and there's one recurring gag that references a certain controversy from before. You see, despite so much acclaim from critics and fans alike, "Once Upon a Time" drew the ire of many with its irreverent treatment of martial arts legend Bruce Lee, portrayed by Mike Moh in the film. Even Lee's family weighed in on the matter, objecting to how easily Cliff puts the arrogant Lee in his place during a fight scene on the set of a film. Not surprisingly, "Cliff Booth" returns to this fraught topic on several occasions, and it certainly feels like a meta-textual jab at the real-world backlash from before.

Was this in poor taste, or is Tarantino merely poking fun with a self-deprecating running joke? Moviegoers will undoubtedly have strong takes on either side. Here's where we land on our second go-around with this little debate.

You know, there's something to be said for addressing an uncomfortable matter head-on instead of dancing around it — or even ignoring it altogether. Quentin Tarantino has never been one to shy away from a fight, a trait he happens to share with washed-up stuntman Cliff Booth. As a master cinephile, a brilliant bartender, a hit with the ladies, and a ruthless killer all in one, the power fantasy of it all isn't exactly hard to see in "The Further Mis-Adventures of Cliff Booth." Perhaps that's what made the Bruce Lee moment in "Once Upon a Time in Hollywood" stand out all the more (a fight that almost ended very differently). It's one thing for Cliff to be the coolest person alive in his world, and quite another for him to intrude rudely upon our own.

So how does "Cliff Booth" address this elephant in the room? Although the incident is brought up on a number of occasions in passing, Yahya Abdul-Mateen II's buzzy actor Rodrick "Hot Rod" Harrison is positively fixated on it once he meets Cliff — a fact that also seems to catch the eye of talent manager Karen Crosby (Elizabeth Debicki). Intriguingly, Cliff himself almost seems embarrassed by the attention, perhaps self-reflexively brushing it off as the brash actions of a young man. Maybe, like Tarantino himself, he's a little older and a little wiser and less apt to repeat the same mistakes.

Less generously, the fact that this fight is emphasized so much in "Cliff Booth" could make the exact opposite point. It's treated almost like a legend or a myth in this world, giving Cliff a dangerous sense of aura and mystique that belies his charming appearance.

Either way, this is just one ingredient tossed into a much larger stew. "The Further Mis-Adventures of Cliff Booth" may struggle to reach the transcendent heights of "Once Upon a Time in Hollywood," but isn't that part of its point, too? "What is Cliff Booth without Rick Dalton?" Quentin Tarantino's script seems to be asking. Not dissimilarly, is a Tarantino movie with David Fincher at the wheel really a Tarantino movie anymore? Who can say how much ownership any of us have over our own art — or our lives in general?

Perhaps that's why "Cliff Booth" feels so much colder and mean-spirited than its predecessor. Although both films build to violent climaxes, the one in "Hollywood" is shot to be much more over-the-top and darkly funny than the one here. Before, Rick Dalton and Cliff were fighting to preserve a very specific way of life — one that the Manson family threatened to overturn. Here, Cliff very easily could've avoided the bloodshed with killers Eddie (Matt Groove) and Fernando Verdusco (JB Tadena), but he pursues this anyway out of ... ego? Principle? A sense of right and wrong? Cliff is a man of both simplicity and contradictions, so perhaps it's only fitting that the filmmaking style reflects this through Fincher's very different approach, as well.

That's where the repeated jabs about the Bruce Lee fight come in, as a moment that Cliff has no choice but to own. "Cliff Booth" is a movie about our attempts to define our own stories — whether it be himself, the troublemaking TV star Charlie Peters (Scott Caan), or Karen. And maybe Bruce Lee, too.

"The Further Mis-Adventures of Cliff Booth" is now playing in limited theaters.

Steelers-Broncos preview: What to expect from Denver’s offense, defense in Week 12

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Despite a dramatic exit from starting quarterback Aaron Rodgers that has catapulted the Steelers into becoming the NFL's biggest story ahead of Thanksgiving, Pittsburgh still has a game to play on Friday against one of the league's best teams: The 8-2 Denver Broncos, currently the No. 2 seed in the AFC.

If the Steelers can stay focused on their Black Friday matchup amid the drama, as McCarthy said the team would, what kind of opponent can Pittsburgh expect in Week 12?

If they squint their eyes, Steelers fans might find a lot of similarities between their own struggling offense and this Broncos unit. Mike McCarthy and Sean Payton's offensive systems have plenty of overlap, including an affinity for 11 personnel groupings and a passing game that relies heavily on the quick passing game to the short areas of the field. The Broncos might have had more luck in the scoring department (23.6 PPG) than the Steelers (21.4 PPG), but Denver has actually been more ineffective by some traditional and advanced metrics alike. It's been a tumultuous season for Denver, who started the season with new offensive coordinator Davis Webb as a play caller, but then, all too predictably, had Sean Payton reclaim those duties in Week 5.

Denver has struggled mightily on the ground this season. They've eclipsed 100 yards rushing in a game in just four games, and rushed for 82 or less in six others. Philosophically, they don't favor one rushing scheme heavily, instead dabbling pretty equally in man (25%), gap (20%), outside zone (23%), and inside zone (20%) concepts. J.K. Dobbins — who Steelers fans should be familiar with from his time in Baltimore — started the season as Denver's lead back, but has significantly lost playing time over the last month. His share of offensive snaps fell to 23% in Week 7 and has gone down each week since, to the point that he took only one snap in Week 11. This despite the Broncos placing R.J. Harvey on injured reserve ahead of their Week 8 contest. Now Dobbins is suspended, with Payton announcing the suspension just 24 hours after telling reporters: "J.K. is going to be super important for what we're doing in this stretch."

While Dobbins and the Broncos game hasn't performed particularly well, part of why Dobbins' playing time began to decrease was the emergence of rookie Jonah Coleman. Dobbins still holds the team lead in rushing yards (324) entering the week, but Coleman has nearly caught him (302) in just six games played.

Coleman is a relatively sound blocker in pass protection and has decently reliable hands for a running back, which has allowed him to stay on the field in passing situations. On the ground, Coleman is a decisive, violent runner who is able to break tackles with his impressive contact balance. He has more lateral quickness than speed, but he has good vision and seems advanced in his reads for a rookie runner. He's coming off his most productive game as a pro, rushing 24 times for 111 yards and three touchdowns and adding four receptions for 18 yards in Week 11. It's worth noting this game against the Raiders, the second-worst rushing defense in the league, but he's the type of runner who wears down a defense and makes it hard to get off the field late in the game.

That is easily the most exciting element of the Broncos' offense. In the passing game, Bo Nix is one of the more frustrating quarterback watches in the league. Nix has played somewhat similarly to recently benched Aaron Rodgers.

Like in Pittsburgh, Denver's wide receivers are struggling to fill up the stat sheet. The team traded a first-round pick (No. 30), a third-round pick (No. 94), and a fourth-round pick (No. 130) in the 2026 NFL Draft to acquire Jaylen Waddle from the Miami Dolphins — and then signed him to a three-year, $84.8 million extension — but have not worked him into the game plan in a way you'd expect, having surrendered that much capital. Waddle does lead the team in receptions (33), targets (58), and yards (398) through 10 games, but those are fairly disappointing per-game averages considering the team gave up assets like they were acquiring one of the league's most dynamic weapons. Perhaps Steelers fans can relate given D.K. Metcalf's struggles since arriving.

But it isn't just Waddle. No Bronco is averaging more than Waddle's 39.8 receiving yards per game, and only tight end Nate Adkins (3) has more touchdowns than Waddle (2). Receivers Courtland Sutton (266 yards), Pat Bryant (221), and tight end Evan Engram (243) have had moments, but no Broncos pass-catchers are producing consistently. Bo Nix has simply been that bad.

Per Next Gen Stats, Nix has averaged the NFL's lowest completion percentage (42.9%) and yards per attempt (4.5) against blitzes in the NFL. His 14 turnover-worthy throws ranked eighth-most in the NFL. Nix has thrown multiple touchdowns in a game just three times this season, never exceeding two in a game. He's failed to reach 200 yards passing in four games this season, and has exceeded 222 passing yards just three times.

Nix also isn't running as much as he has in previous years. That's likely due to the ankle injury he suffered in last year's playoffs, but it's still a remarkable turn. After scrambling 83 times across his first two seasons, Nix has scrambled just nine times this season. Overall, he has 121 rushing yards on 43 attempts and just two touchdowns, on pace to be the least effective rushing season of his career. He's also fumbled nine times this season, after fumbling just six times across the two prior seasons.

Still, much like this current version of Rodgers, Nix can still unleash an impressive throw during the course of a game. He is exactly the type of quarterback who can put a drive together late if an opponent's offense doesn't put the Broncos away late and lets them hang in the game.

The Broncos' offense is mostly bad. Can the Steelers' offense, now turning to Mason Rudolph, put up enough of a fight against Denver's defense to win this game? To be determined.

RB: The Bronco defense's poor yardage numbers don't really mesh with their strong reputation and status as a near-top-10 unit in points allowed. It's an imperfect group, sure, but still a dangerous defense to face late in the year — especially if you're a Steelers team that's been going through some, uh, issues as of late.

Denver hasn't allowed a 30-point game since Week 1, with a defense that has closed out a number of tight games in a six-game winning streak. Advanced metrics have been kinder than traditional statistics regarding the Broncos' defense, which has largely impressed despite facing a murderer's row of offenses (Chiefs twice, Jaguars, Rams, 49ers — even the dangerous Panthers and Raiders). It's also worth noting that Bo Nix and co. have the third-least plays per game on the other side of the ball, which has led to Denver's defense spending more time on the field.

The Broncos' defense starts with its front, which currently ranks fifth in the NFL in sacks this season with 31, exactly one ahead of Pittsburgh.

Defensive end Zach Allen is one of the most underrated D-linemen in the NFL, with his 34 pressures this season being good enough for third-most at his position per Next Gen Stats. Teammate Malcolm Roach has also emerged as one of the best pass rushing defensive tackles in the league with a career-high five sacks in 2026.

And on the edges, Nik Bonitto (five sacks) and Jonathon Cooper (2.5 sacks in four games) are a high-end duo, with Bonitto being one of the fastest rushers in the league while Cooper is a dangerous complement with his power.

According to Next Gen Stats' trends page, the Broncos are the fourth-best team in the league this season at generating pressure with just a four-man rush. But Denver still blitzes quite a bit, with a 35.4% blitz rate that ranks ninth in the NFL. Denver has generated the league's fourth-highest pressure rate this season (39.4%).

The Broncos' attacking front does leave them somewhat susceptible to chunk plays in the run game. Denver has allowed the seventh-most 10-plus yard runs this season (34), along with the fourth-most yards after contact per run (3.42), per NGS. As a team, Denver has 79 missed tackles on the year, tied for the most in the NFL this season.

Linebacker Alex Singleton has been having a career year in terms of tackles (a whopping 118 through 10 games) to go along with three sacks and six stuffs. But in coverage, he's been one of the worst in the NFL this season, allowing an 83% catch rate and 423 passing yards as the nearest defender this season. Denver as a whole has given up the most receiving yards (494) to opposing running backs this season, something that Pittsburgh should look to exploit with Jaylen Warren on Friday.

But in the secondary, Denver has a talented group, led by All-Pro corner Patrick Surtain II and two underrated starters in Riley Moss and playmaking slot Ja'Quan McMillian, who recorded a remarkable 31-yard pick six last week against the Raiders.

According to NGS, Surtain has aligned in press on a whopping 27.3% of his coverage snaps this season. Expect some close, physical coverage on D.K. Metcalf on Black Friday.

Still, Denver's coverage tendencies are difficult to pin down. The team uses man coverage 29% of the time per NGS, which is slightly above league average, but remains one of NFL's most diverse teams in terms of scheme usage. The Broncos are primarily a single-high coverage team, majoring in cover 1 and cover 3, but there's also a good amount of split-high, with Denver using cover 2, 4 or 6 around 35% of the time.

It's worth noting the Broncos' secondary hasn't been fully healthy much this year. McMillian is the only starter who has played in every game. Denver got one starting safety back in the lineup in the hard-hitting Talanoa Hufanga last week, and will add the other in Brandon Jones this week against Pittsburgh — the first time this defense has been fully healthy for a while. (Defensive end Sai'vion Jones and safety Taylor Rapp, both backups, have been ruled out of Friday's game).

The overall point? Don't let the Broncos defense's yardage totals fool you. This is a good group, with one of the best pass rushes and secondaries in the league. Sure, Mason Rudolph is replacing Aaron Rodgers in the lineup this week for the Steelers, but Rudolph has had some struggles with pocket navigation and turnovers in the past, with 11 interceptions and 13 sacks in his past 11 games (most coming in his six starts in that span).

If the Steelers can get Jaylen Warren and Rico Dowdle in space, they could see some success, but this isn't a great matchup for the Pittsburgh offense — with or without the drama leading up to the game.

6 Fantasy Books That Tried to Be Harry Potter

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Feel free to connect with him or check out his work. He's everywhere — Upwork, YouTube, Spotify, SoundCloud, Collider, LinkedIn, Instagram.

On my 11th birthday, I kept checking the door all day. I knew, deep down, that no owl was coming, but a small part of me still hoped. When the day ended with nothing but cake and a few cards, I remember I was broken. I suspect half the kids who grew up with J.K. Rowling’s books had a version of that same quiet heartbreak.

Publishers understood that feeling very well. Once Harry Potter turned into a worldwide craze, they spent years hunting for the next magical school, the next chosen child, the next trio of best friends. Some of those books found their own magic, while others were like cheap copies of Hogwarts. I’ve ranked them by how hard they tried to be Harry Potter and failed miserably.

Callum Hunt has been warned his whole life to stay away from magic. His father insists that the Magisterium, the underground school for young mages, is dangerous. So when 12-year-old Call is called in for the entrance exam, he does his best to fail it on purpose. Unluckily for him, a powerful master picks him anyway, and soon he’s living in caves, learning to control the elements, and making two close friends, Aaron and Tamara.

Fans spotted the problem quickly. A boy with a dark secret, a perfect golden friend, and a smart girl who keeps them both in line feels very much like Harry, Ron, and Hermione. Cassandra Clare, who started out writing Harry Potter fan fiction, and Holly Black knew this, and The Iron Trial cleverly plays with those expectations. The final twist about Call’s past flips the “chosen one” idea on its head, which saves it from being a straight copy.

Twelve-year-old Sophie Foster has never fit in. She’s a genius who’s already in high school, and she can hear everyone’s thoughts, a secret she’s kept from her family forever. Then a mysterious boy named Fitz shows up on a museum field trip and tells her the truth. She isn’t human at all. She’s an elf, and she belongs in a hidden world called the Lost Cities.

Much like Harry stepping into Diagon Alley, Sophie gets whisked off to Foxfire, a magical academy where she learns she’s special in ways even the elves don’t understand. Keeper of the Lost Cities has the outsider hero, the hidden world, and the mysterious figures pulling strings in the background. Still, Shannon Messenger builds a sparkly, colorful world of her own, and the series has grown into a long-running favorite with readers who’ve stuck around for more than ten books.

Every four years, two children disappear from the village of Gavaldon, never to return. Sophie, pretty and pink-loving, dreams of being taken so she can become a fairy-tale princess. Her best friend, Agatha, is gloomy, sarcastic, and just wants to be left alone. When both girls are snatched away to the School for Good and Evil, there’s a hilarious mix-up. Sophie lands in Evil, and Agatha gets dumped in Good.

The School for Good and Evil stands out because Soman Chainani isn’t copying Hogwarts so much as poking fun at fairy tales. Yes, there are houses, rival schools, and strict teachers. But the real fun lies in watching both girls question what “good” and “evil” even mean. Netflix turned it into a 2022 film, with Sophia Anne Caruso as Sophie, Sofia Wylie as Agatha, and Charlize Theron and Kerry Washington as their teachers.

Four worlds and four completely different ideas of what a good life looks like. This is where you belong, what it would ask of you, and what you would get back in return.

You belong somewhere old enough that the mountains have names and somebody can still tell you who gave them. Middle-earth is for people who think the right thing is worth doing even when it will not be finished in their lifetime.

You want the sea, a crew, and absolutely nobody telling you where you are allowed to go. The Grand Line is the world for people whose main ambition in life is finding out what is over there.

You would rather be feared and useful than liked and useless. The Continent is a world where the monsters are the straightforward part of the job and the people are where it all gets complicated.

You want the myths to be real and loose in the actual world, and you want to find out at about thirteen that you were never anywhere near as ordinary as everybody kept telling you.

Fletcher is an orphan raised by a blacksmith in a tiny mountain village. One night, while reading a strange old book, he accidentally summons a demon, a small fire salamander he names Ignatius. Within hours, he’s forced to flee home, and he ends up at Vocans Academy, a school where nobles train to become battlemages. Naturally, the rich kids make sure he knows he doesn’t belong.

Orphan boy, hidden talent, snobby bullies, and a magic school, The Novice ticks off a lot of familiar boxes. Even so, it has an interesting backstory. Taran Matharu began posting the story online on Wattpad when he was in his early twenties, and it gathered millions of reads before landing a book deal. The demon companions added a Pokémon-style charm, and the looming war with orcs gives the series a darker edge than most Hogwarts lookalikes.

On the night Septimus Heap is born, the seventh son of a seventh son, the midwife announces that the baby is dead. That same night, his father finds a newborn girl abandoned in the snow and takes her home. Ten years later, the Heap family learns their adopted daughter is a hidden princess. Meanwhile, a nameless army boy known only as Boy 412 gets swept up in their adventures, and readers slowly realize who he really is.

Whenever I picked up Magyk, I couldn’t help thinking of a baby with a famous destiny being hidden away for his own safety. Angie Sage borrows Harry’s lost-child setup, then adds a cranky wizard named Marcia, plenty of quirky spells, and odd spellings like “Magyk” itself. To be fair, the series has its own cozy, funny personality. But the “special boy who doesn’t know who he is” core is pure Potter.

Quentin Coldwater is a gloomy 17-year-old in Brooklyn who has spent his whole life obsessed with a series of children’s books about a magical land called Fillory. Then, out of nowhere, he’s invited to take an exam at Brakebills, a secret college for magic in upstate New York. He passes. But instead of finding happiness, he discovers that magic is hard, boring, and doesn’t fix his loneliness at all.

Unlike the others on this list, The Magicians doesn’t just borrow from Harry Potter; it answers it. Lev Grossman basically asked, “What if Hogwarts students grew up, got bored, and started drinking too much?” The result is darker, adult, and often uncomfortable. Many people called it “Harry Potter for grown-ups.” It later became a TV series from 2015 to 2020, starring Jason Ralph as Quentin and Olivia Taylor Dudley as Alice, and it earned a loyal, passionate fan base of its own.

Forget ‘Outlander,’ Netflix’s 25-Episode Fantasy Thriller Is a Near-Perfect Binge

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Romance has always been an exceedingly popular genre, but one can argue that more cultural phenomenon-level television shows are enjoying their moment in the sun than ever before. Take modern offerings like Outlander, Bridgerton, and Heated Rivalry, all of which took the world by storm during the last decade. Their individual success comes back to the same collective reason why small-budget rom-com movies once consistently reigned over the box office, and why the assorted sub-genres under romance's umbrella remain some of the highest-selling novels in the world. At the genre's best, its immersive nature lends itself to supporting the thematic priorities for which many underrepresented demographics hunger for: empowerment, escapist wish-fulfillment, the revelatory tangle of intimacy, and harmless theatrical melodrama.

Romance's increased mainstream prevalence extends, albeit on a smaller scale, to A Discovery of Witches. Based on New York Times bestselling author Deborah Harkness' All Souls book series, the 2018 small-screen adaptation surged in popularity once all three seasons hit Netflix in 2024. Although A Discovery of Witches doesn't revolutionize the conventions surrounding fantasy romance, it does excel at creative pivots, memorable interludes, and the TV version of what it feels like to devour a luscious gourmet meal.

A Discovery of Witches fuses elements of supernatural romantasy, historical drama, and conspiracy mystery thriller. Opening in the Gothic-tinged halls of modern-day Oxford University before spreading across the luxurious United Kingdom and European countrysides, Harkness follows Diana Bishop (Teresa Palmer), a historian, scientist, Ivy League professor, and an orphaned witch.

Four love stories, four completely different ideas of what it means to be somebody's person. This is the one that matches how you actually fall, and what it would ask of you.

You do not fall for strangers. You fall for the person you have already been talking to for years, which is the slowest and by some distance the most durable way anybody has found to do this.

You want a partner, not an audience. Somebody who wants their own life as badly as you want yours, and who would never ask you to be smaller to make the relationship easier.

The intensity was never the problem. The secrecy is. You are drawn to a love that lives entirely in private, and to the particular ache of wanting somebody you are not supposed to want.

You do not do this cautiously. When it happens for you it is immediate and total and it rearranges everything, and you would not accept a smaller version of it.

Diana has steadfastly avoided practicing any magic, even for convenience's sake, since her parents' unsolved murders. She also rejects associating with the larger community, aside from her loving aunts (Alex Kingston and Valarie Pettiford). Of course, her best-laid plans go right out the window as soon as an unexpected discovery throws her into the crosshairs of a brewing war spearheaded by malignant narcissists, not to mention an enigmatic and unfairly handsome vampire named Matthew Clairmont (Matthew Goode).

If you don't respond to what's essentially the vampire-and-witch version of Romeo and Juliet with enthusiastic approval, then A Discovery of Witches might glide headfirst into abundant silliness. If you approach it with the right mindset, however, its sincerity and self-awareness are key to the entertainment factor. The series treats the story seriously without taking itself too seriously. It's the kind of comfort-watch indulgence where the cheesiness tastes as scrumptious as the world's best and biggest nacho platter — two top-notch forbidden love stories for the price of one, with all the pleasing aesthetics (velvet shadows, billowing fog, envy-inducing period costumes, steamy rendezvous teetering on the knife's edge between desire and danger) and emotional roller-coaster loops that are required (a frail social order, kidnappings, near-death experiences, sworn enemies becoming in-laws).

Overall, A Discovery of Witches' premise, payoff, apocalyptic impetus, and personal stakes all nicely dovetail. Harkness staunchly honors the internal structure and mechanics of her worldbuilding, braiding together historical figures from Elizabethan-era England, alchemy, rumored witchcraft, and Wiccan spirituality with her own magic system and a biology-based supernatural mythology. The metaphors for prejudice and segregation can lean simplistic and heavy-handed, but in select cases — as long as the execution isn't gratingly clumsy or doesn't perpetuate harmful stereotypes — it's more responsible to lightly, loosely reckon with applicable subtext than ignore its implications.

Stay tuned for more yearning in the Scottish Highlands.

The All Souls books began as a trilogy before Harkness expanded the series into five novels and counting. If there's any justice, A Discovery of Witches' rights-holders will stage a continuation sooner rather than later. The found-family dynamic between the stellar character actors (familiar faces abound for fans of My Lady Jane, Rome, and Game of Thrones) is too good to waste. The same goes for Palmer and Goode's thrumming chemistry, especially since Diana's judicious academic expertise, steel-lined backbone, and emotional vulnerability make her a blazing beacon of well-defined, take-no-prisoners heroism. Until a revival graces our screens, A Discovery of Witches' three seasons offer all the beguiling decadence of a wrapped-and-ribboned Christmas gift. Sometimes, we crave the cozy simplicity of a thrill that's capable of flashing its sharp incisors.

All three seasons of A Discovery of Witches is now available to stream on Netflix.

Jake Gyllenhaal’s Apple TV Thriller Officially Returns Without Him — But That’s OK

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Jake Gyllenhaal served as the lead in the first season of a hit crime thriller on Apple TV. The actor even went on to win his first Primetime Emmy for his performance in the show. However, in the same series' season 2, Jake Gyllenhaal has now been replaced with another lead. As surprising as this may sound, there is good reason behind his absence from the show's new season.

Based on Scott Turow's novel of the same name, Apple TV's Presumed Innocent serves as the second on-screen adaptation of its source material. Long before the show, the book was adapted into a 1990 movie that starred Harrison Ford. Instead of merely emulating the book and the movie's story, the Apple TV show carefully reimagines with new twists and turns.

In season 2, however, the show is not based on the original novel's existing sequels. Instead, it is all set to take the anthology route and replace its entire season 1 roster. Owing to this, Jake Gyllenhaal, too, has been replaced with another promising lead who has also won an Emmy in the past. While Gyllenhaal's absence will likely be felt, Presumed Innocent season 2 already seems incredibly promising.

Rachel Brosnahan headlines Presumed Innocent season 2. The upcoming installment will have no narrative connections with season 1. It is set to follow a completely new story based on Jo Murray's Dissection of a Murder. Brosnahan stars Leila Reynolds, who is an ambitious early career defense attorney.

Presumed Innocent season 2's central conflict begins when Leila lands her first murder case after the accused defendant, Jack Millman, specifically requests her as his legal counsel. Personal stakes are revealed when Leila's husband, District Attorney Julian Kessler, turns out to be the prosecutor.

Jake Gyllenhaal's character in Presumed Innocent season 1, Rusty Sabich, was portrayed as a prosecutor in Presumed Innocent season 1 before he became a murder suspect himself. Season 2 is changing this perspective by having Brosnahan play a defense attorney, Leila. Like Sabich, however, Leila, too, will also have to confront her marital and family issues while striving to win the central murder case.

After Jake Gyllenhaal, Rachel Brosnahan seems like the perfect lead for Presumed Innocent. She, too, has previously won a Primetime Emmy Award for her performance in The Marvelous Mrs. Maisel and has also gained a new wave of fame after playing Lois Lane in Superman. Presumed Innocent season 2 now gives her the opportunity to put her acting range on full display.

Apart from Jake Gyllenhaal, Presumed Innocent's season 1 also starred many other incredibly talented actors, like Chase Infiniti, Peter Sarsgaard, Renate Reinsve, Bill Camp, and Ruth Negga, among others. It looks like season 2 is all set to match its predecessor's roster. The Apple TV show's season 2 has paired Rachel Brosnahan with three-time Emmy winner, Matthew Rhys.

Rhys has previously delivered unforgettable performances in other legal thrillers like Perry Mason. He is also coming fresh off his Emmy win for Apple TV's Widow's Bay.

Presumed Innocent season 2's cast also includes other heavy-hitters, like two-time Emmy winner Courtney B. Vance, Fiona Shaw, Jack Reynor, John Magaro, Lesley-Ann Brandt, and Judith Light. It looks like there are absolutely no weak links in the Apple TV series' upcoming installment.

Jack Gyllenhaal's performance in Presumed Innocent season 1 set an incredibly high bar for the series. In season 2, the Apple TV series is all set to raise the bar even higher and potentially cement itself as one of the biggest legal thrillers of our time.

An eight-episode limited series  based on the New York Times bestselling novel of the same name by Scott Turow. Starring Gyllenhaal in the lead role of chief deputy prosecutor Rusty Sabich, the series takes viewers on a gripping journey through the horrific murder that upends the Chicago Prosecuting Attorney’s office when one of its own is suspected of the crime. The series explores obsession, sex, politics, and the power and limits of love, as the accused fights to hold his family and marriage together.

Sam Rockwell’s New Spy Thriller Officially Says Goodbye to 1,000 Theaters

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The last time Sam Rockwell worked with Martin McDonagh, he ended up winning an Academy Award for his performance in Three Billboards Outside Ebbing, Missouri, having previously worked with McDonagh on Seven Psychopaths. There were another nine Oscar nominations for McDonagh's work four years ago when he reunited Colin Farrell and Brendan Gleeson for The Banshees of Inisherin, and this year, it's the turn of Rockwell and John Malkovich to play two men whose relationship is explored through McDonagh's own distinctive brand of dark comedy. Unfortunately, if you've been planning to see the movie over Thanksgiving weekend, it's about to become considerably more difficult to find a theater showing it.

According to the latest theater count figures from The Numbers, McDonagh's Wild Horse Nine has officially lost 1,000 theaters heading into its fourth weekend of release, reducing its domestic theatrical footprint by exactly 50%. Having opened in 1,100 theaters on November 6, the movie expanded to 2,175 locations in its second weekend, but it will now be playing in 100 fewer theaters than it was on opening day.

Wild Horse Nine is alos nearing the $20 million milestone, having made $18.31 million domestically to date, with $2.46 million of that coming last weekend, when its grosses fell by 46%. The movie was averaging $1,231 per theater over the weekend, although that figure dropped to just $192 on Monday. With Thanksgiving bringing a particularly competitive weekend for theaters, McDonagh's movie has now lost more than 54% of its locations since the second weekend of its release.

McDonagh takes us to Chile in 1973, where two CIA agents are sent to Easter Island shortly before the military coup. For Malkovich's Chris, who's spending an increasing amount of time contemplating his own mortality, there's quite a bit of history between himself and Rockwell's Lee to reckon with, and things become rather more complicated for the two men when Chris befriends a pair of students on the island.

Written and directed by McDonagh, the movie features Malkovich (Con Air) as Chris Carlisle, Rockwell (Moon) as Lee Chesterfield, Steve Buscemi (Boardwalk Empire) as MJ, Mariana di Girolamo (Ema) as Monica, Ailín Salas (The German Doctor) as Alicia, Tom Waits (Seven Psychopaths) as Jim, and Parker Posey (The White Lotus) as Marge.

Hong Kong: Fire Anniversary Underscores Growing Repression

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Click to expand Image A memorial near the site of the deadly fire at the residential complex at Wang Fuk Court in Tai Po, Hong Kong, December 2, 2025. © 2025 Ng Han Guan/AP Photo

(London) – The Hong Kong government should ensure meaningful accountability for the devastating fire that killed 168 people one year ago and end its suppression of peaceful criticism, Human Rights Watch said today.

On November 26, 2025, Hong Kong’s deadliest fire in decades engulfed seven residential high-rise buildings at Wang Fuk Court, Tai Po. The authorities initiated an independent investigation and arrested three dozen people implicated in crimes. The Housing Minister resigned. But the authorities have also threatened and arbitrarily arrested those engaging in criticism and activism concerning the fire.

“The Hong Kong government responded to the horrific Wang Fuk Court fire by using Beijing’s tactics of shutting out the voices of the victims and promising an elusive accountability,” said Elaine Pearson, Asia director at Human Rights Watch. “The authorities need to recognize that the only way to prevent similar tragedies in the future is to allow people to speak out safely and hold those responsible fully accountable.”

The authorities have engaged in apparent acts of repression of free expression in the weeks ahead of the anniversary:

The media reported in November that the police made inquiries with Catholic churches planning masses to commemorate victims of the fire.A church in Tai Po subsequently removed references to the fire from its announcement and instead described the service as a mass for “the brothers and sisters who died in the past year;” hundreds nevertheless attended.A priest at another Catholic church posted on Facebook that three police officers had inquired about a planned memorial mass marking the anniversary, even though the church had not publicly announced the service.On November 22, a group working with migrant workers who survived the fire cancelled a book launch and drama event scheduled ahead of the anniversary, citing “unforeseen circumstances.” On November 23, the online news platform Boom News said it would remove some videos featuring interviews with survivors, quoting the whistleblower Edward Snowden on “censorship and fear.” The Boom News founder, Tang Ho-wing, had been arrested in September for alleged “sedition” for reporting on another politically sensitive anniversary.

Police Commissioner Joe Chow said police routinely asked organizers about upcoming public events, while decisions on whether to proceed “rested with the organizers themselves.”

Amid continued restrictions on freedom of expression and peaceful assembly in Hong Kong, Hong Kong communities around the world have planned commemorations of the fire.

Deep concerns surrounding the 2026 anniversary follow a year-long government crackdown on criticism, reporting, and civil society-organizing related to the fire. Immediately following the fire, hundreds of people gathered near Wang Fuk Court to donate and distribute supplies to victims, but as the volunteer effort grew, police told them to leave and government workers took over the site.

Authorities targeted people calling for accountability. Just four days after the fire, national security police reportedly arrested a university student, Miles Kwan, for “seditious intent” after he initiated an online petition calling for an independent investigation into the fire, and two others on suspicion of “inciting hatred against the government.”

Hong Kong and Beijing authorities subsequently accused “foreign forces” and “anti-China destabilizing forces” of using the fire to “incite hatred against the government.” Within hours of these announcements, two prominent online commentators, Ellie Yuen and Hailey Cheng, who had been frequently quoted in foreign media, said they would stop reporting on the fire.

National security police also arrested the online commentator Wong On-yin in December after he planned to hold a news conference about the Wang Fuk Court fire and discussed on YouTube his subsequent questioning by police. The authorities prosecuted him for allegedly obstructing a national security investigation and publishing “seditious material.” He remains in custody awaiting a verdict in January 2027.

Between April and May 2026, the authorities barred Wang Fuk Court residents from displaying banners from their homes or being accompanied by journalists as they were briefly permitted to return to their apartments to retrieve belongings.

Also in May, the authorities arrested the former Wang Fuk Court owners’ committee member Kong Cheung-fat and his wife on allegations of fraud and money laundering. Kong had joined other owners in seeking a meeting to discuss insurance and other post-fire issues. While out on bail, he said he would no longer give interviews or comment publicly. Some residents said his arrest made them more cautious about speaking publicly.

An independent committee, set up by Hong Kong Chief Executive John Lee, published a report on October 29 that found wide-ranging regulatory failures across four government bodies, including inadequate oversight of renovation work and fire safety, weaknesses in building maintenance regulation, and supervisory failures by contractors and other professionals. That day, Lee and senior officials formally apologized to the victims of the fire and their families while promising to implement “systemic reforms.” Housing Secretary Winnie Ho later stepped down from office while the authorities arrested 35 people in connection with the fire and possible corruption in the renovation project.

“An apology and promises of reform are no substitute for ensuring that people can freely question and criticize the authorities,” Pearson said. “The Hong Kong government should act on the independent committee’s recommendations and provide adequate compensation and other support for victims and their families.”

7 Most Universally Loved Fantasy Movies Of All Time, Ranked

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Fantasy movies have a peculiar advantage over almost every other genre: they can make somewhere you've never been feel like a place you miss. A fictional kingdom can become as familiar as the neighborhood you grew up in, especially when a piece of music announces that it's time to go on an adventure again.

That's part of what makes the best fantasy movie worlds so difficult to leave behind. Their appeal survives changing special-effects technology and the fashions of whichever decade produced them. Some become even more rewarding with age, revealing details that escaped us when we first encountered them.

The phenomenon is particularly apparent with the Harry Potter movies that feel different when you watch them as an adult. Childhood excitement gives way to a more complicated appreciation of the characters and the dangers surrounding them. That experience extends far beyond Hogwarts, particularly with fantasy films that have become beloved fixtures of popular culture.

But there's an important distinction between the greatest fantasy movies ever made and the most universally loved. A critically acclaimed film can be difficult, divisive, or demanding, while a beloved favorite can inspire extraordinary affection without winning a single major award. The rarest achievements manage both.

This ranking considers critical reception alongside sustained audience enthusiasm, cultural impact, rewatchability, and appeal across generations. No movie can literally please everybody, and this isn't a ranking based strictly on review scores or financial success. The higher placements go to films whose popularity has endured and whose emotional appeal reaches beyond devoted fantasy fans.

These seven films have earned remarkably lasting affection, even if deciding which deserves the top spot invites a little friendly disagreement.

Tim Burton's Edward Scissorhands looks like a fairy tale someone accidentally dropped into American suburbia. Behind the pastel houses and manicured lawns is a lonely young man with blades instead of fingers. The neighbors initially celebrate him, then turn suspicious when his differences become inconvenient. Johnny Depp delivers one of his most affecting performances, relying on expressions and gestures to communicate Edward's desperate desire to belong. His tentative relationship with Kim (Winona Ryder) gives this wonderfully strange fantasy its emotional center.

The film's melancholy makes it particularly memorable, especially when paired with Danny Elfman's haunting score. Burton's visual imagination is unmistakable, from Edward's gothic mansion to the unforgettable sight of snow falling from his ice sculptures. Among Johnny Depp's most memorable performances, Edward remains unusually sympathetic. Its bittersweet conclusion gives the movie a narrower emotional appeal than the more celebratory adventures ranked above it, but that sadness is also what makes it so enduring.

A chocolate river, an everlasting gobstopper, and a man who seems completely uninterested in making children feel safe. What more could anyone want from a fantasy movie? Mel Stuart's Willy Wonka & the Chocolate Factory has delighted audiences for generations without losing its wonderfully unsettling personality. Gene Wilder's performance is the movie's greatest attraction, effortlessly switching between charm and quiet menace. His Willy Wonka is unpredictable, frequently hilarious, and impossible to imagine being played quite the same way by anyone else.

Charlie Bucket (Peter Ostrum) provides the heart, with his impoverished family circumstances making the golden-ticket discovery particularly satisfying. The factory itself is a marvel of practical effects and imagination, while its songs have remained part of popular culture for decades. Although some of its darker moments can frighten younger viewers, the film's combination of childhood wish fulfillment and eccentric humor has made it remarkably difficult to outgrow. Even after learning what's behind every door, Wonka's factory still seems capable of surprising its visitors.

Choosing a single Harry Potter movie for a list about universal affection is practically an invitation to an argument. The first installment introduces Hogwarts with an irresistible sense of discovery, while the later movies deliver some of the franchise's biggest emotional payoffs. Nevertheless, Alfonso Cuarón's Harry Potter and the Prisoner of Azkaban arguably strikes the strongest balance between exceptional filmmaking and audience affection. The introduction of Sirius Black (Gary Oldman) and Remus Lupin (David Thewlis) gives Harry's world a more complicated emotional dimension, while the terrifying Dementors suggest that childhood innocence is gradually disappearing.

Cuarón also changes how Hogwarts feels. The castle becomes more lived-in, the changing seasons matter, and the young actors finally have room to develop beyond their established character traits. Even the elaborate time-travel sequence remains one of the franchise's most satisfying narrative achievements. Some fans will always favor the wonder of Sorcerer's Stone, which makes choosing a definitive favorite difficult. Yet Prisoner of Azkaban demonstrates why the series continued to resonate with viewers as they grew older: its magic became more compelling as its characters matured.

Few filmmakers possess Hayao Miyazaki's extraordinary ability to make impossible things feel entirely natural. In Spirited Away, a young girl enters a mysterious world, her parents transform into pigs, and she's forced to find employment in a bathhouse populated by spirits. Somehow, this starts to seem like a perfectly reasonable predicament. Chihiro is an unusually compelling protagonist because she possesses no extraordinary powers or heroic destiny. Her gradual transformation from a frightened child into someone resourceful and compassionate gives the film an emotional foundation that transcends its Japanese folklore influences.

Miyazaki's imagination is breathtaking, although the movie's quieter passages are equally important. The famous train journey across the water is particularly beautiful, allowing viewers to absorb this strange world without another dramatic confrontation. Its Academy Award for Best Animated Feature helped cement its international standing, while its continuing popularity has introduced generations of viewers to Studio Ghibli. Spirited Away is more unconventional than the three films ranked above it, but its emotional clarity makes even its strangest creations feel wonderfully human.

Somewhere over the rainbow, Judy Garland helped establish the language of cinematic fantasy. Nearly nine decades later, The Wizard of Oz remains astonishingly accessible, with an influence extending far beyond the generations that originally watched it in theaters. The ruby slippers, Emerald City, and Yellow Brick Road have become universally recognizable cultural symbols, while Dorothy's companions remain among cinema's most endearing characters.

Yet the movie's longevity isn't simply the result of its historical importance. Dorothy's predicament is timeless: she feels misunderstood, dreams of escaping her ordinary existence, and eventually discovers that what she wanted was closer than she realized. The Scarecrow, Tin Man, and Cowardly Lion are equally appealing because their individual insecurities are so recognizable. Their journey reveals that the qualities they desperately seek are things they've been demonstrating all along.

Among the greatest fantasy movies of the 20th century, The Wizard of Oz occupies an almost untouchable position. Its visual imagination was groundbreaking, and its music continues to resonate across generations. In terms of sheer cultural recognition, it arguably deserves the top spot. However, this ranking also considers the personal attachment and enthusiasm that inspire viewers to return repeatedly, which gives the final two films a slight advantage.

"As you wish." Two words are enough to remind countless moviegoers why Rob Reiner's The Princess Bride has maintained such extraordinary affection since 1987. Adapted by William Goldman from his own novel, the film follows Westley (Cary Elwes) as he attempts to reunite with Buttercup (Robin Wright). Along the way, he encounters an eccentric collection of characters, including Mandy Patinkin's revenge-driven Inigo Montoya and André the Giant's wonderfully gentle Fezzik.

The movie is frequently hilarious, although its willingness to embrace sincere romance is equally important. Reiner understands that audiences can recognize the absurdity of fairy-tale conventions while still becoming emotionally invested in them. The framing device, in which Peter Falk reads the story to his skeptical grandson, cleverly acknowledges the prejudices viewers might bring to an old-fashioned adventure. It's an almost perfect illustration of the film's ability to win over reluctant audiences.

Unlike many fantasy productions, The Princess Bride doesn't rely on spectacular special effects or elaborate worldbuilding. Its considerable charm comes from wonderfully drawn characters and dialogue that only gets funnier with familiarity. There's a reason it belongs among the most comforting fantasy movies ever made. In terms of sheer rewatchability and audience affection, it has arguably the strongest claim to being universally loved. Only one movie surpasses its combination of critical admiration, enduring popularity, and emotional impact on an enormous scale.

Peter Jackson faced an extraordinary challenge when he adapted J.R.R. Tolkien's The Lord of the Rings. He needed to introduce a vast fantasy world without overwhelming newcomers, satisfy readers who had imagined Middle-earth for decades, and convince audiences to follow an enormous adventure across three movies. With The Fellowship of the Ring, he accomplished something remarkable: he made Middle-earth a place millions of viewers wanted to visit again and again.

The opening scenes in the Shire establish the film's emotional appeal long before the adventure becomes dangerous. Hobbiton feels wonderfully lived-in, Gandalf's arrival resembles a reunion with an old friend, and Frodo's departure carries genuine sadness. Elijah Wood gives Frodo an appealing vulnerability, while Ian McKellen finds warmth beneath Gandalf's considerable authority. Even Sauron, one of fantasy cinema's most formidable villains, rarely needs to appear physically to exert his influence.

The sequels feature larger battles and a more definitive conclusion, but The Fellowship of the Ring possesses something neither can recreate: the excitement of entering Middle-earth for the first time. Jackson's film combines extraordinary critical acclaim with mainstream success and the kind of devoted fandom that spans generations. The Princess Bride may be easier to revisit on a casual afternoon, but Fellowship inspires an equally powerful affection while immersing viewers in an entire world. A quarter-century after its release, the invitation to join the Fellowship remains almost impossible to refuse.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.
